6. Design Principles

6.1 Validate Once, Trust Forever (Trust Boundary Principle)

  • Statement: All untrusted input traversal (bounds, alignment, pointer targets, recursion depth, segment limits) occurs in one atomic validation pass in ser-decode. Post-validation accessor reads are unconditionally safe — no re-check, no Option wrapping, no panic.
  • Tradeoffs: Validation cost is upfront and measurable; accessor hot-path is branchless. Schema-valid but semantically incorrect data (wrong enum value for app logic) is outside scope — semantic checks are caller responsibility.
  • Operational: Validation rejection emits SerError + ser_telemetry::validation_rejection counter with error variant tag. Latency histogram separates validation time from access time.
  • Violation consequence: Skipping validation and casting unvalidated buffer bytes → UB via misaligned read, OOB pointer dereference, or crafted amplification loop. Memory corruption; security vulnerability. CVE-class defect.

6.2 Zero-Copy by Default, Copy-on-Demand Explicit

  • Statement: decode() returns Reader<'a,T> borrowing from the source buffer. No field access allocates heap memory or copies bytes. Deep copy (to_owned() / canonicalize()) is opt-in and always named explicitly.
  • Tradeoffs: Caller must keep source buffer alive as long as any Reader derived from it lives — enforced by Rust lifetime 'a. Cannot mutate in place without a separate Builder.
  • Operational: Heap allocation count during decode must be zero (enforced by allocator shim in test harness). Any regression triggers CI failure.
  • Violation consequence: Adding implicit Vec::new() or String::from() inside an accessor quietly invalidates the zero-copy contract, degrades latency, and defeats the cache-locality rationale.

6.3 Layout Determinism (Schema → Layout is a Pure Function)

  • Statement: Given the same Schema, ser-layout produces byte-identical field offsets, data-section sizes, pointer-section sizes, and alignment on every platform, language, and invocation. Wire endianness is fixed little-endian.
  • Tradeoffs: No platform-specific "native layout" optimizations; layout cannot be tuned per-target without changing the schema hash, which breaks compatibility.
  • Operational: SchemaHash embeds layout parameters; a schema-hash mismatch at decode triggers SerError::IncompatibleSchema before any data is read. Golden layout fixtures in ser-layout CI assert bit-exact output.
  • Violation consequence: Non-deterministic layout → reader and writer disagree on field offsets → corrupt reads with no error (data corruption, not crash). Hardest class of bug to detect in production.

6.4 Append-Only Schema Evolution

  • Statement: Published field ids and their offsets are permanent. New fields are appended (new FieldId, new offset). Removed fields leave reserved holes with schema-recorded default values. Union arms are append-only. No reuse of retired ids.
  • Tradeoffs: Data section can accumulate holes; canonical mode zeros them. Old readers skip unknown fields; new readers use defaults for absent fields. Schema size grows monotonically.
  • Operational: ser-evolve enforces these rules as a pre-commit/CI gate; violations emit SchemaEvolutionError with field-id diff. Schema registry tracks version lineage.
  • Violation consequence: Reusing a field id changes the semantic meaning of existing wire data → silent data corruption in old readers; violates the fundamental compatibility contract.

6.5 Bounded Resource Usage (Amplification Defense)

  • Statement: All traversal is bounded: max recursion depth (default 64), max total bytes across all segments (default 512 MiB), max segment count (default 512), max list element count per list. Far pointers may not form cycles.
  • Tradeoffs: Legitimate deeply nested or very large messages require explicit limit overrides via DecodeConfig. Default limits are conservative; change requires deliberate override.
  • Operational: Limit violations → SerError::DepthExceeded or OutOfBounds. Counters in ser-telemetry. Fuzz corpus must include crafted amplification payloads; CI fuzzer runs until no new coverage in last N seconds.
  • Violation consequence: Unbounded traversal → malicious wire input causes O(exponential) CPU or O(N²) memory use → DoS. See Cap'n Proto security advisory 2018.

6.6 Canonical Encoding as a First-Class Feature

  • Statement: encode(canonical=true) produces byte-identical output for logically-equal messages: fixed field ordering (by field id), zeroed padding, no dead pointer space, no trailing garbage. Canonical form enables content-addressed dedup, HMAC signing, and equality without schema-aware comparison.
  • Tradeoffs: Canonical mode requires one extra zeroing pass over padding bytes (≈5–15% encode overhead). Non-canonical mode is faster and equally valid on the wire.
  • Operational: ser-bench measures canonical vs non-canonical encode latency separately. ser-tool diff uses canonical form for comparison. Schema hash is computed over canonical schema encoding.
  • Violation consequence: Non-deterministic canonical output → HMAC signing breaks on re-encode; dedup yields false negatives; equality checks require deep field-by-field comparison.

6.7 Observability by Default (No Silent Failures)

  • Statement: Every error path emits a typed SerError variant. Every validation rejection, schema mismatch, and encode failure increments a named counter in ser-telemetry. Encode/decode/validate latency histograms are always active (zero-allocation metrics path).
  • Tradeoffs: Telemetry adds a few nanoseconds per operation (atomic counter increment + histogram bucket update). Opt-out is not supported for error counters; latency histograms can be disabled via feature flag.
  • Operational: Dashboards alert on validation_rejection_total > threshold (likely attack or client bug). schema_mismatch_total rise signals rolling deploy issue.
  • Violation consequence: Silent swallowing of decode errors → caller proceeds on garbage data; operational incidents have no signal; impossible to distinguish corrupt data from empty/null responses.

6.8 Safe Abstractions Over Unsafe Internals

  • Statement: unsafe code is confined to ser-decode validation internals and ser-core pointer arithmetic. Every unsafe block has a // SAFETY: comment documenting the invariant that makes it sound. Public APIs are entirely safe Rust.
  • Tradeoffs: Wrapper overhead is negligible (inlined); safety audit surface is bounded to clearly-marked modules. New contributors cannot accidentally introduce unsafe code in business logic.
  • Operational: cargo geiger tracks unsafe-code count per crate; CI fails if unsafe count increases outside ser-decode/ser-core without a documented exception.
  • Violation consequence: Unsafe in accessor code (outside validated trust boundary) → UB reachable from safe caller code → soundness hole, potential CVE.

6.9 Schema-First, Reflection-Second

  • Statement: The primary API is schema-driven: code-generated typed Reader/Builder accessors from ser-codegen. The reflective API (dynamic field lookup by name/id) is a fallback for tooling and is never on the hot encode/decode path.
  • Tradeoffs: Code generation requires a build step; reflective API has name-lookup overhead. Typed accessors are zero-cost (compile-time offsets); reflective accessors do a hashmap lookup per field.
  • Operational: ser-tool inspect and schema registry use reflective API. Application data paths must use typed generated code. ser-bench reports per-API latency to enforce this.
  • Violation consequence: Defaulting to reflective access in hot paths → 10–100x accessor latency increase; defeats zero-copy performance rationale.

6.10 Endianness Fixed at Little-Endian

  • Statement: All scalar values on the wire are little-endian. ser-encode calls to_le_bytes(); ser-decode validation calls from_le_bytes() before any arithmetic check. No host-endian shortcuts.
  • Tradeoffs: On big-endian hosts (rare, e.g. SPARC, s390x), every scalar read/write incurs a byte-swap. Modern CPUs: single instruction, negligible cost. Portability and interoperability outweigh the marginal big-endian cost.
  • Operational: Cross-endian CI job (QEMU big-endian target) runs round-trip tests. ser-bench big-endian numbers are tracked separately.
  • Violation consequence: Host-endian read on a little-endian wire → garbled scalars; bug manifests only on big-endian hosts; extremely hard to detect in homogenous x86 CI.

6.11 Fail Fast at Encode, Fail Safe at Decode

  • Statement: Encode errors (schema violation, missing required field, layout overflow) are programming errors → panic! in debug, SerError in release, logged. Decode errors (malformed wire data) are environmental → always SerError, never panic, logged + metered.
  • Tradeoffs: Encode panics in debug mode surface schema misuse immediately during development. Decode must never panic regardless of input — panic = DoS from untrusted network input.
  • Operational: #[cfg(debug_assertions)] guards encode panics. Decode path is #[no_panic]-annotated (checked via no-panic crate in CI). Fuzz harness asserts decode() never panics.
  • Violation consequence: Panic in decode path → adversary sends crafted wire data → process crash → availability incident.

6.12 Separation of Schema, Layout, and Wire

  • Statement: ser-schema owns semantic type model (names, ids, types, evolution). ser-layout owns byte-level decisions (offsets, sizes, alignment). ser-encode/ser-decode own wire I/O. No layer reaches into another layer's domain.
  • Tradeoffs: Three-layer indirection adds conceptual overhead. Benefit: layout algorithm can be replaced without touching encode/decode; schema model can gain new type kinds without touching layout.
  • Operational: Layer violations (e.g., encode directly reading field names from schema) are clippy lint violations via #[doc(hidden)] on cross-layer internals. Architecture fitness tests assert ser-encode has no direct dep on ser-schema internals.
  • Violation consequence: Cross-layer coupling → layout changes require encode/decode changes; parallel schema/layout evolution becomes impossible; maintainability degrades to monolith.

6.13 Immutable Readers, Mutable Builders

  • Statement: Reader<'a,T> is read-only, borrows the source buffer, and is Copy. Builder<'a,T> owns its arena and supports mutation until finish(). There is no in-place mutation of a decoded buffer — mutations require building a new message or using ser-encode copy-and-modify.
  • Tradeoffs: No "update in place" convenience API. Benefit: Reader is trivially Send + Sync; builder arena isolation prevents use-after-free on concurrent access.
  • Operational: Reader: Copy + Send + Sync is enforced via static_assertions::assert_impl_all! in ser-decode.
  • Violation consequence: In-place mutation of validated buffer → validation invariants violated post-facto; subsequent accessor reads may be unsound.

6.14 Deterministic Build and Reproducible Artifacts

  • Statement: ser-codegen output (generated Rust files) is deterministic: same schema → byte-identical generated source. No timestamps, random suffixes, or non-deterministic iteration order in output.
  • Tradeoffs: Requires sorting all generated items by field id before emission. HashMap iteration must be replaced with BTreeMap in codegen.
  • Operational: CI compares generated code against committed golden files; diff = failure. SchemaHash is included in generated file header for traceability.
  • Violation consequence: Non-deterministic codegen → spurious diffs in version control → noise in code review; schema hash mismatches if codegen is re-run between encode and decode.

6.15 Explicit Message Framing, No Magic Bytes

  • Statement: Message framing (segment count, segment sizes, root pointer) is an explicit header in ser-runtime. No magic bytes, no version field in the header itself (version is in the schema hash, exchanged out-of-band). Framing is minimal and fixed-size calculable.
  • Tradeoffs: No self-describing format detection — you must know you have an encoded message to decode it. Magic bytes would add 4–8 bytes per message (rejected as non-zero overhead for tiny messages).
  • Operational: ser-tool can detect likely framing errors and suggest schema/version. Framing errors surface as SerError::Truncated or OutOfBounds.
  • Violation consequence: Ambiguous framing → attempts to decode non-message data silently; adds a validation miss in error handling.

7. System Invariants

Shorthand: elsewhere in this document (phase theory anchors, Hard Questions, Mental Models, Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model) invariants are cited by a compact I# token rather than the full §7.N heading. Each token used anywhere in this document resolves to exactly one invariant below:

ID Cited As Resolves To
I1 Canonical byte-identical output for logically-equal messages §7.8 Canonical Form Stability
I2 Layout is a deterministic pure function of schema §7.3 Layout Determinism
I3 Arena allocation is monotonic (bump pointer) and isolated per Builder; reset, not freed, between reuses §7.12 Builder Arena Isolation
I4 Field offsets never change retroactively once published §7.6 Append-Only Field Ids
I5 No buffer cast or Reader construction without a completed validation pass §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary
I6 Traversal depth bounded (MAX_NESTING_DEPTH = 64) §7.5 Recursion / Depth Bound
I7 Total validation traversal work bounded (MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS) §7.9 Segment Size Cap
I8 Text fields are UTF-8 validated before &str construction §7.11 UTF-8 Validity of Text Fields
I9 Segment count bounded (MAX_SEGMENTS = 512) §7.9 Segment Size Cap
I10 Total message size bounded (MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES = 512 MiB) §7.9 Segment Size Cap
I11 Differential correctness vs. reference implementations verified in CI Testing & Verification Strategy — Differential Testing

7.1 Zero-Copy Read — No Allocation, No Copy in Accessor Path

  • Invariant: After decode() returns Ok(Reader<'a,T>), every field accessor (reader.field_name()) performs zero heap allocations and zero byte copies. Data words are read directly from buf at computed offset; pointer-typed fields return a derived Reader<'a,U> also borrowing buf.
  • Why: Core value proposition; violating it makes this project a slower Serde.
  • Enforced: Allocator shim in test mode counts allocations; CI test asserts allocation count = 0 for all field reads. Reader implements no Clone that allocates (only bitwise Copy).
  • Detection: Allocation count test failure; heap profiler (dhat) in integration tests.
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::decode_alloc_bytes counter must stay at 0 post-decode; any nonzero value is a CI-blocking metric.
  • Recovery: No runtime recovery — allocation in accessor is a code defect. Fix: remove offending code; never add Vec/String construction to accessor path.
  • Debug: cargo test --features test-alloc-count enables shim; grep accessor impls for Vec::, Box::, String::, clone().

7.2 Validation Trust Boundary — No Unvalidated Buffer Cast

  • Invariant: ser-decode only casts (ptr::read_unaligned or equivalent) bytes from buf after the validation pass has confirmed: offset is in-bounds for buf.len(), alignment requirement is satisfied for the target type, pointer target is within a declared segment, recursion depth < limit.
  • Why: Casting unvalidated bytes → UB (misaligned read on strict-align arch, OOB access → SIGSEGV or worse, silent data corruption).
  • Enforced: All unsafe casts gated behind ValidationGuard token (linear type pattern — cannot construct without running validation). ValidationGuard is #[must_use] and not Clone.
  • Detection: ASAN + MSAN in CI catch misaligned/OOB reads. cargo miri runs unit tests to detect UB.
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::validation_rejection{error} counter per SerError variant.
  • Recovery: Validation failure → return Err(SerError::*) to caller; buffer is not accessed further. Caller decides: log, drop, retry with different schema.
  • Debug: Enable RUST_LOG=ser_decode=trace for per-pointer validation trace. Reproduce fuzz corpus item with ser-tool inspect --no-validate (dry-run, prints rejected pointer).

7.3 Layout Determinism — Schema Hash Covers Layout Parameters

  • Invariant: For any Schema S, ser-layout::compute(S) returns identical Layout (offsets, data_words, ptr_words, align) on every invocation, every platform, every build. SchemaHash is computed after layout; it changes iff layout changes.
  • Why: Reader and writer must agree on byte offsets without exchanging layout at runtime. Any non-determinism = silent field misread (data corruption, not error).
  • Enforced: Golden layout tests: checked-in JSON fixture per schema; CI asserts compute(schema) == fixture. Cross-platform CI (x86_64, aarch64, big-endian QEMU) runs same fixture check.
  • Detection: Layout golden test failure; SchemaHash mismatch at decode (triggers IncompatibleSchema error).
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::schema_mismatch_total — spikes indicate rolling deploy with incompatible schema versions.
  • Recovery: IncompatibleSchema error → caller must obtain compatible schema version and retry. Schema registry provides version lookup. No partial-decode fallback.
  • Debug: ser-tool schema diff <schema_a> <schema_b> shows field-level layout diff including offsets and hash components.

7.4 Pointer Safety — All Pointers Resolve Within Declared Segments

  • Invariant: Every struct/list/far pointer in a validated message resolves to a byte range entirely within a declared segment's bounds. Far pointers reference a SegmentId present in the message's segments vec. No pointer escapes the message boundary.
  • Why: Out-of-message pointer dereference = OOB memory access = UB/CVE. Cycles = infinite loop in traversal = DoS.
  • Enforced: Validation pass: for each pointer, compute target_byte = seg.start + offset * 8; assert target_byte + size <= seg.end. Visited pointer set (bloom filter or bitset) detects cycles.
  • Detection: SerError::BadPointer on validation failure. ASAN catches any missed OOB.
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::bad_pointer_total{type} — distinguishes struct/list/far pointer violations.
  • Recovery: BadPointer → reject message, emit error. No partial decode. Log source segment id and offset for forensics.
  • Debug: ser-tool inspect --hex <msg.bin> prints pointer table with resolved targets; marks invalid pointers with [INVALID].

7.5 Recursion / Depth Bound — Traversal Depth Never Exceeds Limit

  • Invariant: During validation, traversal of nested struct/list pointers increments a depth counter. If depth >= config.max_depth (default 64), validation aborts with SerError::DepthExceeded before following any further pointer.
  • Why: Maliciously crafted pointer cycles or deep nesting → stack overflow or O(exponential) traversal CPU use → DoS.
  • Enforced: ValidationState { depth: u32 } threaded through all recursive validation calls. Not a global; re-entrant safe.
  • Detection: SerError::DepthExceeded. Fuzz corpus must include max-depth messages; CI asserts validation terminates.
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::depth_exceeded_total — nonzero in production signals either attack or misconfigured limit.
  • Recovery: Return error; optionally allow caller to increase limit via DecodeConfig::max_depth. Log message source IP/service for abuse detection.
  • Debug: Add tracing::trace!(depth, "pointer follow") in validation pass; RUST_LOG=ser_decode=trace shows traversal tree.

7.6 Append-Only Field Ids — No Id Reuse, No Offset Retroactive Change

  • Invariant: Once a FieldId appears in any published schema version, it retains its wire type, offset, and default for all future versions. It may be deprecated (marked deprecated = true) but the id-offset binding is permanent.
  • Why: Old readers use baked-in offsets from code-generated accessors. If an id is reused for a different type, old readers silently misinterpret data.
  • Enforced: ser-evolve::check_compatibility(old: &Schema, new: &Schema) iterates all field ids in old; asserts each is present in new with identical ty, offset, default. CI gate on schema change PRs.
  • Detection: SchemaEvolutionError::FieldIdReused { id, old_ty, new_ty } or OffsetChanged { id, old, new }.
  • Telemetry: Schema evolution check results logged to schema registry audit log with schema hash pair.
  • Recovery: Reject schema publish. Author must append a new field id for the new semantic. Old id must be marked reserved.
  • Debug: ser-tool schema evolve-check <v1.szs> <v2.szs> produces field-level diff table.

7.7 Little-Endian Wire Encoding — No Host-Endian Leakage

  • Invariant: All scalar values (integers, floats, enum discriminants, offsets, counts) stored in a segment are in little-endian byte order. ser-encode always calls T::to_le_bytes(); ser-decode validation and accessors always call T::from_le_bytes().
  • Why: Big-endian host writing host-endian wire data → undetected interop failure with little-endian reader (silent data corruption, not an error).
  • Enforced: Clippy custom lint banning to_be_bytes() and to_ne_bytes() in ser-encode/ser-decode. Cross-endian CI job.
  • Detection: Round-trip test on QEMU big-endian: encode on big-endian, decode on little-endian, assert equality.
  • Telemetry: No runtime metric needed (static property); caught at compile/test time.
  • Recovery: Code fix only — no runtime recovery path.
  • Debug: Hex dump wire bytes; compare field offset bytes with expected little-endian encoding of the test value.

7.8 Canonical Form Stability — Same Logical Message → Same Bytes

  • Invariant: encode(msg, canonical=true) is a pure function of the logical message content and schema. Padding bytes zeroed, fields ordered by ascending FieldId, no trailing garbage, no dead pointer space. Re-encoding a canonically decoded message yields the same bytes.
  • Why: Content-addressed storage, HMAC signing, and byte-equality comparison depend on this. Nondeterministic canonical form → false cache misses, signature verification failures.
  • Enforced: Golden canonical encoding tests: fixed logical value → fixture bytes; CI asserts. Property test: encode(canonical, msg1) == encode(canonical, msg2) whenever msg1 and msg2 are logically equal.
  • Detection: Canonical golden test failure; signature verification failure in user's system (indirect).
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::canonical_encode_latency_ns histogram — track overhead vs non-canonical.
  • Recovery: Code defect; fix canonical encoder. No runtime mitigation.
  • Debug: ser-tool diff --canonical <a.bin> <b.bin> shows field-level diff of canonical forms.

7.9 Segment Size Cap — Total Wire Message Size Bounded

  • Invariant: Total bytes across all segments in a message ≤ DecodeConfig::max_message_bytes (default 512 MiB). Segment count ≤ DecodeConfig::max_segments (default 512). Total words visited during validation traversal ≤ MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS (= max_message_bytes / 8 = 64 Mi words), bounding validation work independently of segment count. Checked before any segment content is accessed.
  • Why: Allocation amplification: a message header claiming 2^32 bytes forces allocating/reading a huge buffer before any data validation. Cap prevents memory exhaustion DoS.
  • Enforced: Framing parser in ser-runtime reads segment size table from header and sums sizes before allocating or reading segment data. Returns SerError::OutOfBounds or Truncated if exceeded.
  • Detection: Error at framing parse stage; no segment content touched. ser_telemetry::message_size_exceeded_total.
  • Recovery: Reject message. Caller may increase limits if legitimate large message expected. Log source and size for capacity planning.
  • Debug: ser-tool framing <file.bin> prints segment table; shows claimed vs actual sizes.

7.10 Schema Hash Exchange — Decode Only Proceeds With Matching Hash

  • Invariant: When schema-hash checking is enabled (default), decode() compares the SchemaHash embedded in the message (or provided by caller) against the SchemaHash of the local schema. Mismatch → SerError::IncompatibleSchema before any field access.
  • Why: Without hash check, a reader with schema v2 may silently misread a message written with schema v1 if field ids overlap but types differ.
  • Enforced: ser-runtime::decode() performs hash check as first step. Opt-out via DecodeConfig::skip_schema_hash_check = true (only for schema migration tooling).
  • Detection: SerError::IncompatibleSchema { expected, got }. ser_telemetry::schema_mismatch_total.
  • Recovery: Caller retrieves correct schema version from schema registry, retries decode. Or uses ser-evolve::migrate() if schemas are evolution-compatible.
  • Debug: ser-tool schema hash <schema.szs> prints hash; compare with hash extracted by ser-tool framing <msg.bin>.

7.11 UTF-8 Validity of Text Fields

  • Invariant: Any WireType::Text field in a validated message contains valid UTF-8 bytes. The validation pass calls std::str::from_utf8() on the text byte range. Invalid UTF-8 → SerError::BadPointer (reused for wire-level invalidity).
  • Why: Reader::text_field() returns &'a str; Rust's str invariant is valid UTF-8. Bypassing this check violates Rust's safety model for str construction.
  • Enforced: Validation pass includes UTF-8 check for all Text-typed pointer targets. // SAFETY: comment in accessor cites "validated by ValidationPass".
  • Detection: SerError::BadPointer with context message "invalid UTF-8 in Text field". ser_telemetry::utf8_validation_failure_total.
  • Recovery: Reject message. Sender must ensure text fields are valid UTF-8 before encoding (enforced at encode time via &str parameter type).
  • Debug: ser-tool inspect --hex shows raw bytes of text fields; pipe through xxd to identify invalid byte sequences.

7.12 Builder Arena Isolation — No Cross-Builder Pointer Sharing

  • Invariant: A Builder<'a,T> allocates all its data within its own Arena. Nested struct/list builders are always sub-allocations within the same arena. Pointers from one Builder's message never reference bytes in a different Builder's arena.
  • Why: Cross-builder pointers → invalid far-pointer targets when either message is serialized independently → BadPointer at decode or worse, silent misread.
  • Enforced: Arena carries a unique ArenaId; sub-builders borrow &mut Arena and inherit the same ArenaId. Pointer encoding checks that target ArenaId matches source. Debug-mode assertion.
  • Detection: Debug assertion failure during encode. In release mode, cross-arena pointers manifest as BadPointer at decode time.
  • Telemetry: ser_telemetry::cross_arena_pointer_attempt_total (debug builds only).
  • Recovery: Programming error — fix builder usage. No runtime recovery.
  • Debug: Enable RUST_LOG=ser_encode=debug; builder logs arena id on each allocation.

7.13 Monotonic Schema Versioning — Version Numbers Never Decrease

  • Invariant: Each published schema version carries a monotonically increasing since_version: u32. Fields added in version N have since_version = N. Schema registry rejects a push where the new schema version ≤ the current latest version for that type name.
  • Why: Forward compat depends on readers knowing "this field was unknown to me (I'm version M < N), use default". If versions are non-monotonic, readers cannot determine which fields to default.
  • Enforced: Schema registry enforces monotonic version at push time. ser-evolve::check_compatibility also validates that all new fields have since_version > max(old.fields.since_version).
  • Detection: SchemaEvolutionError::NonMonotonicVersion. Registry push returns HTTP 409.
  • Recovery: Author must use next available version number. No rollback of version counter.
  • Debug: ser-tool schema versions <type_name> lists published versions with hashes and field counts.

8. Domain Model — Serialization Wire Format & Zero-Copy Runtime

8.1 Overview: Core Entities and Relationships

Schema ──owns──> TypeDef* ──owns──> Field*
   |                |                  |
SchemaHash      Layout             FieldId, Offset, WireType, DefaultVal, since_version
   |
   └──> evolution rules (append-only, reserved holes)

Message ──owns──> Segment* ──contains──> raw bytes (words, pointers)
    |                                        |
  root: Pointer                   data-section | pointer-section
    |
  Reader<'a, T> ──borrows──> Segment.bytes (lifetime-tied, zero-copy)
  Builder<'a, T> ──writes──> Arena (segment allocator)

Entity inventory:

Entity Cardinality Invariant
Schema 1 per wire format version Identified by SchemaHash; immutable once published
TypeDef N per Schema id unique within Schema; layout is pure fn of TypeDef
Field N per TypeDef FieldId unique within TypeDef; offset non-overlapping
Layout 1 per TypeDef data_words * 8 + ptr_words * 8 = total struct size
Segment 1..N per Message SegmentId contiguous 0..N-1; max count bounded
Pointer embedded in segment Resolves within declared segment; far ptr stays in-message
Reader<'a,T> 0..1 per access path buf lifetime ≥ Reader lifetime; no internal allocation
Builder<'a,T> 1 per encode session Backed by Arena; finalized to Message on seal()

8.2 Wire Format: Segment Layout

MESSAGE FRAMING (little-endian):
 ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ frame header (8 bytes):                                    │
 │   [0..4] segment_count: u32  (number of segments, 1-based) │
 │   [4..8] reserved: u32 = 0                                 │
 ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ segment_sizes table: segment_count * 4 bytes               │
 │   each entry: size in words (u32, little-endian)           │
 │   pad to 8-byte boundary if segment_count is even          │
 ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ Segment[0] bytes  (size_words[0] * 8 bytes)                │
 │ Segment[1] bytes  ...                                      │
 │ ...                                                        │
 └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
root pointer: first word of Segment[0] (always a Struct pointer)

Frame header invariants:

  • segment_count ≥ 1; max enforced limit: 512 segments (amplification defense).
  • Each segment size: max 512 MiB (words = 67,108,864); sum of all segments ≤ 1 GiB per message.
  • Framing is NOT part of zero-copy path — framing header is parsed into Message metadata.
  • Segment bytes after framing are never copied; Reader borrows directly from transport buffer.

8.3 Pointer Encoding

Three pointer kinds, all 8 bytes (one word), little-endian:

STRUCT POINTER (bits [1:0] = 00):
 ┌────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
 │  [63:32] B:u16 │ [31:16] A:u16│ [1:0]=00 off │
 │  ptr_words     │  data_words  │  word offset  │
 └────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘
 bits [31:2]: signed offset in words from end-of-pointer to target struct
 bits [47:32]: data section size in words (A = data_words)
 bits [63:48]: pointer section size in words (B = ptr_words)

LIST POINTER (bits [1:0] = 01):
 ┌──────────┬───────────────┬────────────┐
 │ elem_type│  element_count│ [1:0]=01   │
 │  [2:0]   │  [63:35] u29  │  offset    │
 └──────────┴───────────────┴────────────┘
 bits [31:2]: signed offset in words from end-of-pointer to list data
 bits [34:32]: element size tag (0=void, 1=1-bit, 2=1-byte, 3=2-byte, 4=4-byte, 5=8-byte, 6=ptr, 7=composite)
 bits [63:35]: element count (for composite: total words in list, then per-element tag word)

FAR POINTER (bits [1:0] = 10):
 ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────┐
 │   [63:32] segment_id │ [31:3] off   │
 │                      │ [2]=landing  │
 │                      │ [1:0]=10     │
 └──────────────────────┴──────────────┘
 landing=0: far ptr -> struct/list ptr in target segment at offset
 landing=1: double-far ptr -> two consecutive words in target segment (far-ptr + tag)
 segment_id: SegmentId of target; must be in-message (0..segment_count-1)

NULL POINTER: all 64 bits = 0 → field absent; read returns default value

Pointer invariants:

  • Struct pointer: data_words and ptr_words match TypeDef.layout exactly — validated in pass.
  • List pointer: element_count * elem_size ≤ segment bytes remaining.
  • Far pointer: segment_id < segment_count; offset within segment bounds.
  • Double-far: two target words within same segment, both within bounds.
  • Circular pointers: caught by recursion depth counter (max 64 frames).
  • Self-referential segment[0]→segment[0] cycles: detected by tracking visited (seg, offset) pairs.

8.4 Data Section and Pointer Section Layout

STRUCT MEMORY LAYOUT in segment:
 ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │  data section: data_words * 8 bytes          │
 │  Field values by WireType:                   │
 │    Scalar (Bool..F64, Enum): at bit/byte off │
 │    Union: tag word + active arm field        │
 ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │  pointer section: ptr_words * 8 bytes        │
 │  One 8-byte pointer slot per reference field │
 │  (Struct, List, Text, Bytes, Pointer)        │
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

FIELD PLACEMENT RULES:
  Bool         → 1 bit; packed into data word, bit offset = field.offset
  I8/U8        → 1 byte at byte offset (8-aligned within word)
  I16/U16      → 2 bytes, 2-byte aligned
  I32/U32/F32  → 4 bytes, 4-byte aligned
  I64/U64/F64  → 8 bytes (full data word), 8-byte aligned
  Enum         → 2 bytes (u16 discriminant), 2-byte aligned
  Union        → 2-byte tag (Enum discriminant) + active arm field in same or next position
  Text/Bytes   → pointer section; points to list-of-U8 + NUL for Text
  Struct/List  → pointer section; struct or list pointer

Alignment padding rules:

  • Data section size rounded up to multiple of 8 bytes (1 word).
  • Pointer section size always in words (8-byte multiples).
  • No padding between data and pointer sections beyond word alignment.
  • Intra-word field packing: smaller scalars share a word, packed low-to-high by FieldId.
  • Bool packing: up to 64 bools in one data word; bit offset = FieldId % 64.

8.5 List Encoding

COMPOSITE LIST (structs or unions):
 ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │  tag word: Struct pointer with offset=−1     │
 │    data_words=X, ptr_words=Y, count=N        │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │  element[0]: data section (X words)          │
 │              pointer section (Y words)       │
 │  element[1]: ...                             │
 │  ...                                         │
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

PACKED SCALAR LIST (non-composite):
 element_count * element_size bytes, no padding between elements
 (elem_size from list pointer element tag)

VOID LIST:
 element_count > 0 legal; zero bytes stored; accessor returns ()

TEXT LIST / BYTES LIST:
 List of pointer; each pointer → a list-of-U8 (Text has trailing NUL, counted in length)

List invariants:

  • Composite: tag word data_words/ptr_words match element TypeDef.layout.
  • element_count * (data_words + ptr_words) * 8 ≤ remaining segment bytes from list start.
  • Nested list max depth: 64 (shared with struct recursion counter).
  • Empty list: element_count=0; tag word present; zero bytes follow.
  • Text: UTF-8 enforced at validation; trailing NUL byte present but not counted in user-visible length.

8.6 Schema State Machine

States of a Schema through its lifecycle:

  [DRAFT] ──parse(.szs)──> [PARSED] ──layout()──> [LAID_OUT] ──hash()──> [PUBLISHED]
     |                        |                       |                       |
  parse err              field conflict          alignment err           SchemaHash stable
  -> Err(SchemaInvalid)  -> Err(SchemaInvalid)  -> Err(SchemaInvalid)   -> immutable

  [PUBLISHED] ──evolve(new_schema)──> [COMPAT_CHECK]
                                           |
                              compat? ──> [EVOLVED] (new SchemaHash)
                              incompat? -> Err(IncompatibleSchema)

Schema state transitions (normative):

From Event To Guard
DRAFT parse() success PARSED All TypeIds unique, all FieldIds unique per type
DRAFT parse() failure ERROR Lex/parse error; schema unusable
PARSED layout() success LAID_OUT No overlapping offsets; alignment satisfied
PARSED layout() failure ERROR Field id collision or alignment impossible
LAID_OUT hash() PUBLISHED SchemaHash = xxhash3(canonical schema bytes)
PUBLISHED evolve(new) COMPAT_CHECK New schema provided
COMPAT_CHECK compat pass EVOLVED New fields are append-only, old ids/offsets unchanged
COMPAT_CHECK compat fail ERROR Err(IncompatibleSchema) with diagnostic

8.7 Validation Pass State Machine

The validation pass is the single trust boundary. Operates on raw bytes, produces Reader or error.

INPUT: &[u8] (wire bytes) + Schema

  START
    │
    ▼
  [PARSE_FRAME] — extract segment_count, segment sizes, verify sum ≤ limit
    │ err → Err(Truncated | OutOfBounds)
    ▼
  [VALIDATE_SEGMENTS] — verify each segment: size matches frame table, non-overlapping
    │ err → Err(OutOfBounds | Truncated)
    ▼
  [READ_ROOT_PTR] — word[0] of segment[0]; must be Struct pointer
    │ err → Err(BadPointer)
    ▼
  [VALIDATE_NODE(ptr, depth)] — recursive; depth counter starts at 0
    │
    ├─ struct ptr: check data_words/ptr_words vs schema layout
    │              check target range in segment
    │              recurse each pointer-section slot (depth+1)
    │
    ├─ list ptr:   check element_count * size ≤ segment remaining
    │              if composite: validate tag word; recurse each element (depth+1)
    │              if pointer: recurse each pointer (depth+1)
    │
    ├─ far ptr:    segment_id < segment_count; offset in bounds
    │              if double-far: validate two landing words
    │              continue validation at resolved location
    │
    └─ null ptr:   OK; field will read default
    │
    ▼ (all nodes validated)
  [EMIT_READER] — Reader<'a, T> { buf: &'a [u8], base: root_offset }
    │
  DONE — zero-copy access is now safe

Validation failure modes and error mapping:

Failure SerError Detail
Frame too short Truncated segment_count * 4 + 8 > buf.len()
Segment size overflow OutOfBounds sum of segment words * 8 > buf.len()
Segment count > 512 OutOfBounds amplification guard
Struct range OOB OutOfBounds ptr_target + (data+ptr)*8 > segment end
Misaligned pointer target Misaligned target % 8 ≠ 0
data_words mismatch BadPointer ptr.data_words ≠ schema layout.data_words
ptr_words mismatch BadPointer ptr.ptr_words ≠ schema layout.ptr_words
Far seg_id ≥ count BadPointer segment does not exist
Depth > 64 DepthExceeded recursion/cycle guard
List count overflow OutOfBounds count * size > segment remaining
Unknown union discriminant BadPointer value not in schema enum
Text missing NUL SchemaInvalid UTF-8 list without trailing 0
Far loop (circular) DepthExceeded (seg, offset) revisit detected

8.8 Reader Access Model (Zero-Copy)

Reader<'a, T> borrows buf: &'a [u8]. Accessors perform:
  1. Compute field byte offset: base + data_section_start + field.offset
  2. Cast &buf[off..off+size] to &T directly (valid because validation guarantees alignment + bounds)
  3. Return reference (scalar) or new Reader<'a, SubT> (sub-struct/list)

NO allocation. NO parse. NO copy. One memory access per field.
Absent field (null pointer): returns field.default as DefaultVal (no buffer access).

Reader operations by WireType:

WireType Accessor returns Implementation
Bool bool buf[word_off] >> bit_off & 1
I8..I64 i8..i64 ptr::read_unaligned (LE) or direct cast if aligned
U8..U64 u8..u64 same
F32/F64 f32/f64 bit-cast from u32/u64
Enum EnumType read u16 discriminant; map to variant
Struct Reader<'a, SubT> resolve pointer; return new Reader at target
List ListView<'a, ElemT> resolve list pointer; return slice or iterator
Union UnionReader<'a> read tag word; dispatch to active arm Reader
Text &'a str resolve list-of-U8; cast to str (UTF-8 validated)
Bytes &'a [u8] resolve list-of-U8; return slice
Pointer AnyReader<'a> opaque; caller downcasts with schema type

Lifetime rules:

  • Reader<'a, T> lifetime 'a tied to the original buffer slice.
  • Nested Readers share the same 'a — no sub-lifetime introduced.
  • Compiler enforces: buffer cannot be freed while any Reader alive.
  • Explicit deep-copy: reader.to_owned() → allocates; not zero-copy. Opt-in only.

8.9 Builder / Encode Model

Builder<'a, T> writes into Arena:
  Arena { segments: Vec<Segment>, current: usize, cursor: usize }

  alloc_struct(layout) → Builder<'a, T>:
    if cursor + size ≤ segment.capacity → bump cursor, return builder at offset
    else → alloc new Segment (next SegmentId), write far pointer in current segment

  set_field(field_id, value):
    compute byte offset from layout
    write value at offset (little-endian)

  init_struct(field_id) → Builder<'a, SubT>:
    alloc SubT in current/new segment
    write Struct pointer into parent pointer section

  init_list(field_id, count) → ListBuilder<'a, ElemT>:
    alloc count * elem_size bytes
    write List pointer in parent pointer section

  seal() → Message:
    finalize all segments; fix up far pointers; emit framing header

Builder invariants:

  • Arena never reallocates existing segments (pointers remain valid).
  • Segment growth: new segment allocated when current segment would overflow.
  • Far pointer written immediately when crossing segment boundary.
  • Default field values: zero for scalars (XOR-encoded default: write field.default ^ value).
  • Canonical mode: all padding zeroed; fields written in FieldId order; no dead space.
  • Non-canonical mode: padding unspecified (may contain garbage for performance).

8.10 Union Encoding

UNION in data section:
 ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │  tag word (2 bytes): u16 discriminant         │
 │    offset = union.offset (from layout)        │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │  active arm field: at arm.offset              │
 │    (may overlap with other union arms;        │
 │     only active arm is valid)                 │
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Unnamed union (top-level): tag at data_words[0] bits [15:0]
Named union: tag at layout-assigned offset; arm fields follow

Validation: discriminant value must be in schema enum; unknown arm → BadPointer
Reader: read tag → match arm → dispatch accessor
Builder: set discriminant; zero out overlapping arm bytes before writing active arm
Evolution: new arms added at end; old readers see unknown tag → return default/error

8.11 Schema Evolution Worked Example

Scenario: UserProfile v1 → v2 adding an optional field.

v1 IDL:
  struct UserProfile {   # TypeId = 42
    @0 id:    U64;       # FieldId=0, offset=word0, since_version=1
    @1 name:  Text;      # FieldId=1, ptr_slot=0,  since_version=1
  }
  Layout v1: data_words=1, ptr_words=1

v2 IDL (evolved):
  struct UserProfile {
    @0 id:    U64;       # unchanged
    @1 name:  Text;      # unchanged
    @2 score: F64 = 0.0; # FieldId=2, offset=word1, since_version=2, default=0.0
  }
  Layout v2: data_words=2, ptr_words=1

Forward compatibility (v1 reader, v2 wire):

  • v1 reader: data_words=1 → reads only word0 (id); ignores word1 (score).
  • Pointer: v2 struct pointer has data_words=2; v1 reader treats extra words as padding → safe.
  • v1 reader accesses name (ptr_slot=0) → ptr_words=1 still; offset unchanged → correct.

Backward compatibility (v2 reader, v1 wire):

  • v2 reader: data_words=2; v1 wire has data_words=1.
  • Struct pointer in v1 wire has data_words=1 < schema's 2 → score field absent.
  • Accessor for score: field.offset > actual data section → return field.default (0.0).
  • No error; v2 reader gracefully reads default.

ser-evolve checks (compile-time/registration):

  • All v1 FieldIds present in v2 with same type + offset → OK.
  • New fields have since_version > v1.schema_version → OK.
  • No field removed without reserved hole → OK.
  • IncompatibleSchema if: field type changed, offset changed, id reused for different type.

8.12 Sequence Diagrams

Encode Path

App                Builder<T>           Arena            ser-runtime
 |                    |                   |                  |
 |--init_root(T)----> |                   |                  |
 |                    |--alloc(layout)--> |                  |
 |                    |<--Offset---------|                   |
 |--set(id, val)----> |                  |                   |
 |                    |--write(off,val)->|                   |
 |--init_list(id,N)-> |                  |                   |
 |                    |--alloc(N*sz)---> |                   |
 |                    |<--ListBuilder----|                   |
 |  (fill list elems) |                  |                   |
 |--seal()----------> |                  |                   |
 |                    |--finalize()----> |                   |
 |                    |<--Message--------|                   |
 |--encode(msg)------------------------------> |             |
 |                                             |--frame()--> |
 |<--&[u8] wire bytes--------------------------------------- |

Decode / Zero-Copy Read Path

App           ser-runtime         ser-decode           Reader<'a,T>
 |                |                   |                     |
 |--decode(buf)-> |                   |                     |
 |                |--validate(buf)--> |                     |
 |                |                   |--parse_frame()      |
 |                |                   |--walk_ptrs()        |
 |                |                   |--check_bounds()     |
 |                |                   |--check_align()      |
 |                |                   |--check_depth()      |
 |                |    err? ──────────X Err(SerError)       |
 |                |<--Ok(Reader)------|                     |
 |<--Reader<'a,T>-|                   |                     |
 |                                                          |
 |--reader.get_id()---------------------------------------->|
 |                                          cast buf[off..] |
 |<--u64 (no alloc, no copy)----------------|              |

Schema Evolution Check

ser-evolve
  |
  |--load_schema(v1_hash) -> Schema_v1
  |--load_schema(v2_hash) -> Schema_v2
  |--check_compat(v1, v2):
  |    for each field f1 in v1:
  |      find f2 in v2 by FieldId
  |      assert f2.ty == f1.ty
  |      assert f2.offset == f1.offset
  |    for each f2 new in v2:
  |      assert f2.since_version > v1.schema_version
  |      assert f2.default.is_some()
  |--Ok(CompatReport) | Err(IncompatibleSchema{field_id, reason})

8.13 Malformed / Edge-Case Examples

Case Wire bytes (hex sketch) Validation result
Zero-length message 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 Err(Truncated) — root ptr missing
Segment count = 0 00 00 00 00 ... Err(OutOfBounds) — count must be ≥ 1
Segment size overflow count=1, size_word=0xFFFFFFFF Err(OutOfBounds) — 32 GiB > limit
Struct ptr data_words mismatch ptr says 3, schema says 1 Err(BadPointer)
List count * elem_size overflows count=0xFFFF, size=4, seg=8 bytes Err(OutOfBounds)
Depth 65 deeply nested struct chain Err(DepthExceeded)
Far ptr → self (seg=0, off=0) circular Err(DepthExceeded) after 64 hops
Unknown union discriminant tag=0xFFFF, schema has arms 0..3 Err(BadPointer)
Text missing NUL UTF-8 list, last byte ≠ 0 Err(SchemaInvalid)
Non-UTF-8 text bytes Text field with 0xFF byte Err(SchemaInvalid)
Misaligned struct target ptr offset → odd byte Err(Misaligned)
Empty message (valid) count=1, size=1 word, root=null ptr Ok(Reader) — all fields default
Canonical duplicate identical logical msg, different padding canonical() normalizes; hash equal

8.14 DefaultVal Encoding

DefaultVal per WireType:
  Scalar  → field.default XOR wire_value (XOR-encoded to allow zero-default = zero-wire)
  Text    → null pointer → return "" (empty str)
  Bytes   → null pointer → return &[]
  Struct  → null pointer → return "default struct" (all fields default recursively)
  List    → null pointer → return empty ListView
  Enum    → field.default = discriminant u16
  Bool    → field.default = 0 or 1 (bit)

XOR encoding invariant: zero bytes on wire = field holds its default value.
  e.g. default=5 (U8): write (5 XOR value) at offset; read (5 XOR wire_byte)

8.15 Canonical Encoding

Canonical mode properties:

  • All padding bytes = 0x00.
  • Fields written in ascending FieldId order within each struct.
  • No dead/unreachable segments.
  • List elements in original order (list ordering is app-defined; canonical does not sort).
  • Far pointers minimized: prefer same-segment when possible.
  • Result: SHA-256(canonical_bytes) is stable identity for dedup/caching.
  • Canonical encode is ~10–15% slower than non-canonical (extra zeroing pass).
  • Non-canonical encode: default; padding unspecified; faster.
  • Conversion: Message::canonicalize() → allocates new Arena, re-encodes.

8.16 ser-telemetry Instrumentation Points

Instrumentation point Metric / Span
encode() entry/exit encode_duration_us, encode_bytes
validate() entry/exit validate_duration_us, validate_rejections_total
decode() (post-validate) decode_duration_us (accessor overhead, sampled)
far pointer resolution far_ptr_count (per message)
segment allocation segments_per_message histogram
schema evolution check compat_check_duration_us, incompat_total
arena alloc arena_bytes_allocated
validation error by kind rejection_by_error_kind{OutOfBounds,BadPointer,...}

Tracing: tracing spans wrap encode(), validate(), codegen(). Field-level access NOT traced (too hot; sampling only).

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
ADR-01 Implementation language Rust (edition 2021, MSRV 1.78) C++ (buffer safety), Go (GC + layout limits), Java (GC + layout limits), Zig (ecosystem immaturity) Zero-copy demands lifetime-checked borrows, no GC, precise layout control; safe unsafe encapsulation C++: when integrating with an existing C++ codebase where FFI overhead outweighs safety benefits and the team has deep ASAN-enforced C++ discipline; Go: when throughput targets are relaxed (< 100 MB/s) and schema-first is less critical than rapid iteration
ADR-02 Build system Cargo (workspace) Bazel, Buck2, CMake Cargo is idiomatic; workspace unifies all 12 crates; reproducible with Cargo.lock; no cross-lang compilation needed Bazel/Buck2: when the project is part of a large monorepo with multiple languages (C++, Java, Python) where Cargo does not integrate and build hermeticity across languages is required
ADR-03 Dependency policy Minimal; no default-features; pin exact versions in Cargo.lock Broad dependency tree Zero-copy runtime is security-sensitive; each dep is an attack surface; audit via cargo-deny Broad dependency tree: in a rapid-prototyping context where developer velocity matters more than security auditability and the artifact is not exposed to untrusted network input
ADR-04 Wire endianness Little-endian fixed Big-endian, network byte order, native endian x86/ARM are LE; WASM is LE; fixed endian avoids per-platform branches; mirrors Cap'n Proto Big-endian / network byte order: when interoperating with legacy mainframe (z/OS, SPARC) systems or network protocols (XDR, SunRPC) where the peer controls the wire format and re-negotiation is not possible
ADR-05 Word size 8 bytes (64-bit word) 4-byte word (FlatBuffers), 16-byte, variable Natural 64-bit alignment; pointer encoding fits comfortably; matches Cap'n Proto 4-byte word: in embedded or 32-bit microcontroller targets (Cortex-M) where 8-byte alignment wastes RAM and pointer arithmetic is 32-bit; FlatBuffers' 4-byte word is correct for that regime
ADR-06 Integer encoding Fixed-width LE (no varint in default mode) Varint (protobuf), zigzag, LEB128 Zero-copy requires fixed offsets; varint requires parsing pass; packed varint mode is opt-in Varint/LEB128: when message payloads consist primarily of small integers (< 128) and wire size dominates over decode latency — e.g., mobile battery-constrained environments where bandwidth cost exceeds compute cost
ADR-07 Schema IDL format Custom .szs (ser-schema) JSON Schema, Protobuf .proto, Thrift, Avro IDL, Cap'n Proto .capnp Full control over extension points; evolution rules embedded; no dependency on external IDL compilers Protobuf .proto: when sharing schemas across an organization that already uses the Protobuf ecosystem and toolchain investment amortizes across dozens of services; Thrift: in polyglot environments (Python/Java/C++) with mature Thrift generator infrastructure
ADR-08 Schema hashing xxHash3 (SchemaHash u64) SHA-256, SHA-1, MD5, FNV xxHash3: collision-resistant for non-crypto use; extremely fast; 64-bit sufficient for schema registry SHA-256: when SchemaHash is used as a security boundary (schema as capability token, tamper-evident registry) rather than merely a compatibility identifier — cryptographic strength required for adversarial schema forgery
ADR-09 Async runtime None in core crates; tokio optional in ser-runtime async-std, smol, embassy Core encode/decode is synchronous CPU work; async is transport concern (out of scope); tokio integration opt-in embassy/no-std async: on bare-metal microcontrollers without an OS where synchronous blocking APIs are impossible and embassy's single-executor async is the only viable concurrency model
ADR-10 Memory allocator System allocator (Builder/Arena); jemalloc optional mimalloc, tcmalloc, bump-only Arena allocator for encode is internal; system alloc default; jemalloc feature flag for bench/prod mimalloc: in high-frequency trading or game-server workloads where thousands of short-lived messages per millisecond stress the default allocator's thread-local caches; mimalloc's segment-based design reduces fragmentation dramatically
ADR-11 Concurrency model No shared mutable state in core; Reader<'a,T> is Send+Sync Lock-based global state, Rc Rust type system enforces: Reader is read-only borrow → trivially Sync; Builder is owned → Send Lock-based global state: when a single-threaded event loop (Node.js-style) owns all encode/decode and the simplicity of a global mutable context outweighs the overhead of per-message arena allocation
ADR-12 Logging tracing crate (structured spans + events) log crate, slog, env_logger tracing supports async context; structured fields; compatible with OpenTelemetry; de-facto Rust ecosystem log crate: in no_std embedded targets where tracing's allocations are unacceptable and only a simple key=value log line over UART is feasible
ADR-13 Metrics metrics crate facade + Prometheus exporter statsd, OpenTelemetry metrics SDK, custom metrics facade decouples impl; Prometheus pull model standard for k8s; histogram for latency StatsD: in legacy infrastructure locked to Graphite/Graphite-compatible stacks where Prometheus is not deployed and push-based metrics over UDP are the team's existing operational standard
ADR-14 Testing framework cargo test (unit + integration); rstest for parametric Custom runner, proptest-only rstest parametric covers schema × encoding combinations; cargo test standard; no additional framework dependency Custom runner: in very large codebases (> 10K tests) where cargo test's parallelism model is insufficient and deterministic test ordering across thousands of integration tests requires a custom orchestrator
ADR-15 Property-based testing proptest quickcheck, bolero, manual fuzz tables proptest strategy composition fits schema type hierarchies; shrinking produces minimal counterexamples bolero + libFuzzer: when corpus-guided fuzzing coverage is more important than human-readable shrunk counterexamples — i.e., during security hardening phases where coverage density over the property space matters more than debuggability of failures
ADR-16 Fuzzing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) + honggfuzz AFL++, custom mutators only cargo-fuzz: idiomatic; structure-aware fuzz targets for validation pass; dual-fuzzer increases coverage AFL++: when targeting highly structured binary protocols where AFL's custom mutator framework and coverage-guided splicing produce better corpus diversity than libFuzzer's sequential byte mutations
ADR-17 Unsafe code policy Encapsulated in ser-decode accessor casts only; #[deny(unsafe_code)] on all other crates Pervasive unsafe, safe-only (impossible for zero-copy) Minimal unsafe surface; audit limited to one module; unsafe behind post-validation trust boundary Pervasive unsafe: in kernel drivers or hypervisor code where every operation requires hardware-level control and the trust model already assumes a fully privileged context with no untrusted inputs
ADR-18 Sanitizers AddressSanitizer + MemorySanitizer in CI Valgrind, Dr. Memory cargo test -Zsanitizer=address/memory; catches use-after-free and uninit in unsafe; MSAN catches uninit reads from buffer Valgrind/callgrind: when instruction-count-stable profiling for reproducible performance measurement is required and ASAN's execution overhead (2-3x) makes functional testing impractical at scale
ADR-19 CI/CD GitHub Actions; cargo check, clippy --deny warnings, cargo test, cargo fuzz (short runs), MSRV check Jenkins, CircleCI, self-hosted only GH Actions: free tier sufficient; matrix: stable + MSRV 1.78 + nightly (sanitizers); reproducible via pinned toolchain Self-hosted Jenkins: in air-gapped government/defense environments where internet access from CI is prohibited and GitHub Actions runners cannot be used; or when custom hardware (FPGA, GPU) is required for testing
ADR-20 Benchmarking criterion (statistical), divan (micro) bench stdlib, iai-callgrind, custom timing criterion: confidence intervals + regression detection; divan: low-overhead micro; ser-bench crate drives differential comparisons vs capnproto-rust, flatbuffers, prost iai-callgrind: when the CI environment has high noise (shared runners, variable CPU frequency) making wall-clock criterion measurements unstable; instruction-count-based benchmarks are deterministic regardless of system load
ADR-21 Code generation strategy Proc-macro (ser-codegen) + build.rs integration Runtime reflection only, external codegen CLI only Proc-macro: type-safe generated Reader/Builder at compile time; build.rs: IDE-friendly; reflective fallback for dynamic schemas Runtime reflection only: in schema-less / dynamic environments (plugin systems, REPL tools) where types are discovered at runtime and compile-time codegen is impossible; or for prototyping before schema stabilizes
ADR-22 Schema registry Local file-based + optional HTTP endpoint in ser-tool etcd, Confluent Schema Registry, custom DB Local registry: no infra dependency; HTTP endpoint: optional, no mandatory external service; schema identified by SchemaHash Confluent Schema Registry: in a Kafka-heavy organization where a centralized, highly-available schema registry is already operated and the consistency guarantees of a distributed store are required for rolling schema deploys across hundreds of services
ADR-23 Documentation rustdoc + mdBook for spec GitBook, Confluence, Notion rustdoc: inline with code; mdBook: rendered spec site; both in-repo → versioned with code Confluence/Notion: in organizations where documentation is co-authored by non-engineering stakeholders (product, legal, compliance) who require WYSIWYG editors and cannot contribute to Markdown-in-repo workflows
ADR-24 Licensing MIT OR Apache-2.0 (dual) GPL, BSL, proprietary Standard Rust ecosystem dual-license; maximally permissive; compatible with all downstream uses GPL: when the project is a foundational library and the authors want to ensure all derivative works remain open-source (copyleft); BSL/proprietary: when commercial use must be gated to monetize the runtime
ADR-25 Release engineering cargo-release + semver; crate publish order (ser-core first) Manual publish, custom release scripts cargo-release enforces semver; publish order respects dependency DAG; CHANGELOG.md per crate Custom release scripts: in complex polyglot monorepos where cargo-release cannot orchestrate the interplay between Rust crate publishing and Docker image building, Helm chart versioning, and internal artifact registry promotion
ADR-26 Reproducible builds Pinned rust-toolchain.toml; locked Cargo.lock; deterministic layout Floating toolchain, unpinned deps Layout determinism requirement mandates build reproducibility; bit-identical output across machines Floating toolchain: in open-source libraries that intentionally test against multiple Rust versions to catch regressions early and where exact reproducibility is less important than broad compatibility signal
ADR-27 Security tooling cargo-audit (advisory DB); cargo-deny (license + dep policy); MSRV check Snyk, manual review only cargo-audit: automated CVE scanning; cargo-deny: blocks unapproved licenses; both run in CI on every PR Snyk: in enterprises with centralized security policy enforced across polyglot codebases (Rust + Python + Go + Java) where a single tool scans all ecosystems and integrates with JIRA/ServiceNow ticketing workflows
ADR-28 Error handling thiserror for SerError derive; anyhow in CLI/tool crates only Custom error enums (boilerplate), failure (unmaintained), eyre thiserror: zero-cost derive, no allocation; anyhow: ergonomic context in binaries; never anyhow in library crates eyre+color-eyre: in developer-facing CLI tools where rich, colored error output with spantrace and suggestion text significantly improves debugging ergonomics; not suitable for library error types
ADR-29 Serialization of schema itself Custom binary format (SchemaHash + field table) for registry JSON, CBOR, protobuf Schema wire format must not depend on the serialization library being defined; bootstrap problem JSON: when human-readability of the schema registry is prioritized (e.g., schema files committed to git as part of the API contract, reviewable in code review without tooling); acceptable when schema-registry throughput is low
ADR-30 Packed integer mode Opt-in pack() API producing varint-compressed scalars Always-packed (breaks zero-copy), always-fixed Zero-copy incompatible with varints; packed mode produces compact bytes but requires decode pass; separate API surface Always-packed: in pure network transmission contexts (mobile APIs, IoT telemetry) where wire byte count is the dominant cost and the decode latency budget is relaxed — prost/protobuf wins here
