CRCFailure crc_failures_total > 0 ERROR runbook/storage-integrity.md
RegistryDown schema_registry_errors_total > 10 (1-min window) ERROR runbook/registry.md

SLO Definitions

SLO Target Measurement Window Error Budget
Decode p99 latency < 100 µs 30-day rolling 0.1%
Encode p99 latency < 200 µs 30-day rolling 0.1%
Validation rejection false-positive rate < 0.001% of valid messages 30-day rolling 0.001%
Schema registry availability > 99.9% 30-day rolling 43.2 min/month
CRC integrity (stored messages) < 1 failure / 10^8 reads 30-day rolling per-deployment

Incident Response

  1. Alert fires → on-call acknowledges within 5 min.
  2. Check dashboard for rejection/latency/error pattern; identify affected schema_hash.
  3. Run ser-tool inspect on a captured failing message.
  4. If schema mismatch: freeze schema publish pipeline; run ser-evolve compat-check.
  5. If OOB/corruption: capture raw bytes; file bug with repro; activate dead-letter queue.
  6. Rollback if decode success rate drops > 5% from baseline.
  7. Post-incident: blameless review; update fuzz corpus; add golden fixture.

Runbooks (stored in ser-tool/runbooks/)

  • validation-rejections.md: capture raw bytes, ser-tool wire-diff, identify sender version.
  • schema-evolution.md: freeze publish, run ser-evolve diff, revert or re-assign field ids.
  • latency.md: compare ser-bench baseline, flamegraph, identify regression commit.
  • arena-sizing.md: profile message size histogram, increase ArenaConfig::max_bytes.
  • storage-integrity.md: fsck equivalent, replay from replica, restore from backup.
  • registry.md: check replica, restore from last-known-good snapshot, re-register schemas.

Deployment Topology

  • ser-runtime is a library; no standalone service; deployed as dependency of host services.
  • ser-tool deployed as a CLI binary or sidecar for schema registry + inspection tasks.
  • Schema registry: replicated key-value store (pluggable; etcd / S3 / local file system).
  • Metrics: Prometheus scrape endpoint embedded in host service via ser-telemetry.

Canary Strategy

  • New ser-runtime version deployed to 1% of hosts first; monitor validation_rejections_total and decode_duration_seconds for 30 min.
  • Canary gate: no regression in p99 latency > 5%; rejection rate delta < 0.01%.
  • Automatic rollback if gate fails; alert on rollback.

Rolling-Upgrade Strategy

  • ser-runtime is a library: rolling upgrade = rolling redeployment of dependent services.
  • During upgrade, messages encoded by old version must decode with new version (backward compat guaranteed — see Compatibility & Versioning).
  • Schema hash in framing header allows receivers to detect schema version before decoding.
  • No coordinated cutover required if schema is backward-compatible.

Rollback Strategy

  • Pin Cargo.lock per release; rollback = revert Cargo.lock + rebuild.
  • Schema rollback: re-publish prior schema version to registry; new field ids in rolled-back version remain reserved.
  • Encoded messages on disk/wire remain valid for older readers if backward-compatible (no rollback needed for stored data).

Migration Strategy

  • IDL migration: ser-tool schema-migrate <old.szs> <new.szs> generates a migration report + reserved-hole patch.
  • Data migration: not required for append-only evolution; required only if field semantics change (breaking change — requires new TypeId, not migration).
  • Generated code migration: ser-cli codegen re-run; diff reviewed; schema hash enforced at runtime.

Backup and Restore Testing

  • Schema registry snapshot: automated daily; restore tested weekly in staging environment via ser-tool registry restore.
  • Restore test: load snapshot → decode 1000 golden messages → assert byte-identical output.
  • Fuzz corpus snapshots: included in weekly restore test; cargo fuzz run must complete without new crashes.
  • Golden byte fixtures: ser-bench/fixtures/*.golden decoded on restore; checksum verified.
  • MTTR target for registry: < 15 min from snapshot to service-ready.

Compatibility & Versioning

Wire Compatibility

  • Wire format is versioned via frame_version: u8 in the message framing header (current = 1).
  • Future format versions increment frame_version; old readers reject unknown versions with SerError::IncompatibleSchema.
  • Within a frame version, encoding is layout-deterministic: same schema → same byte layout on all platforms; little-endian fixed.
  • Canonical encoding mode: byte-identical output for logically-equal messages; hashing/dedup safe.
  • Non-canonical (default) encoding: field order guaranteed, padding zeroed, but builder may leave dead space in pointer section (allowed, harmless, not exploitable).

Storage Compatibility

  • Messages stored to disk include schema hash in framing header; ser-runtime can reject or warn on hash mismatch at read time.
  • Optional CRC-32C in framing header; readers with CRC enabled reject frames with CRC failure.
  • Long-term storage: schema must be preserved alongside stored message bytes or in a schema registry; messages are not self-describing beyond the schema hash.
  • No embedded schema in wire format by default (size overhead rejected); schema transmitted out-of-band (registry or pre-shared).

Schema Evolution Guarantees

Operation Backward Compat Forward Compat Notes
Add optional field (new FieldId) Yes Yes Absent field reads default
Add union arm Yes Yes Unknown arm → discriminant preserved
Add enum variant Yes Yes Unknown variant → raw value preserved
Remove field (mark reserved) Yes (reader uses default) Yes Id must not be reused ever
Change field type NO NO Breaking; requires new TypeId
Reorder fields NO NO Breaking; layout changes
Rename field (id unchanged) Yes (wire unaffected) Yes API change only
Change default value Wire compat; semantic break Wire compat Treat as API break; semver minor
Change struct to union NO NO Breaking
  • ser-evolve compat-check enforces these rules; blocks schema publish on violation.
  • Field ids are globally unique per type and monotonically increasing; deleted ids are permanently reserved in IDL as reserved <id>.
  • Schema hash covers field ids, types, and layout; not names or defaults → rename/default changes do not alter hash.

API Stability (Rust crate API)

  • ser-core, ser-schema, ser-layout, ser-decode, ser-encode, ser-runtime: public API follows semver; breaking changes require major version bump.
  • ser-codegen, ser-evolve, ser-tool, ser-cli, ser-bench: semver minor for additive changes; major for breaking CLI/output format changes.
  • #[non_exhaustive] on SerError, WireType, Pointer enums — match arms must include _ wildcard; adding variants is a minor bump.
  • Internal crates (ser-telemetry): public for instrumentation hooks; semver honored.
  • Minimum Supported Rust Version (MSRV): 1.78; MSRV bumps require a minor version increment and changelog entry.

Rolling-Upgrade Guarantees

  • New reader + old writer: guaranteed if schema is backward-compatible (no removed required fields, no field-id reuse).
  • Old reader + new writer: guaranteed if schema is forward-compatible (only added optional fields/arms).
  • Mixed-version cluster: both directions simultaneously safe given above; no coordinated cutover required.
  • frame_version mismatch: old reader returns SerError::IncompatibleSchema; message not silently misread.

Deprecation Policy

  • Deprecated API items marked with #[deprecated(since = "X.Y.0", note = "...")].
  • Deprecated items removed no sooner than 2 major versions after deprecation.
  • Deprecated wire features: announced in release notes; removed only with frame_version increment.
  • Deprecated schema constructs (e.g., legacy union encoding): ser-evolve emits WARN at compat-check time; migration path documented.

Semantic Versioning Policy

Change Type Version Bump
New crate feature, backward-compatible API addition Minor (0.Y.0)
Bug fix, no API change Patch (0.0.Z)
Breaking API change, wire format change, MSRV bump Major (X.0.0)
New wire format version (frame_version increment) Major
Schema evolution rule tightening Minor (additive)
Metric name change / label addition Minor
CLI flag rename / removal Major for ser-cli; Minor if additive
  • CHANGELOG.md required for every release; entry format: [X.Y.Z] — YYYY-MM-DD with Added / Changed / Deprecated / Removed / Fixed / Security sections.
  • Release artifacts: crates published to crates.io; ser-tool binary released as GitHub Release asset for Linux (x86-64, aarch64) and macOS (aarch64).

Migration Guarantees (Cross-Version)

  • Messages encoded with version N readable by version N+1 if schema is backward-compatible (guaranteed by ser-evolve gate).
  • Messages encoded with version N+1 readable by version N if only optional fields added (guaranteed by forward-compat check).
  • No support guaranteed for messages encoded more than 2 major versions ago; frame_version mismatch → explicit error.
  • Generated accessor code tied to SCHEMA_HASH; mixing accessor code from version N with runtime from version N+1 requires same schema hash or explicit re-generation.
  • ser-evolve produces a machine-readable compat report (--output json) for CI integration and audit log.

Testing & Verification Strategy

Unit Tests — Per-Crate Scope

  • ser-core: TypeId/FieldId/Offset round-trip; WireType discriminant values; SerError Display/Debug; DefaultVal coverage per variant.
  • ser-schema: IDL tokenizer edge cases (empty structs, nested unions, max-field-id); schema hash stability across parse reruns; name-collision detection; duplicate FieldId rejection; since_version ordering.
  • ser-layout: offset assignment determinism (same schema → same Layout every run); alignment padding correctness (word-align ptr section, byte-align scalars); data_words + ptr_words overflow guard; union layout (discriminant word position); list stride calculation.
  • ser-encode: Builder fill then read-back per WireType; segment boundary crossing (multi-segment message); far-pointer emission; padding bytes zeroed; canonical flag produces byte-identical output for equal inputs.
  • ser-decode: accessor returns correct value after validation; out-of-bounds → OutOfBounds; misaligned pointer → Misaligned; invalid pointer target → BadPointer; depth-exceeded recursion → DepthExceeded.
  • ser-evolve: append field → old reader returns default; remove (reserved hole) → new reader returns default; union arm add → compat OK; type change → compat Err.
  • ser-telemetry: counter increments on encode/decode/validate; latency histograms bucket correctly; label cardinality bounded.

Integration Tests — Cross-Crate Flows

  • End-to-end: IDL parse → layout → codegen → encode → validate → zero-copy read; assert field values match source.
  • Multi-segment: build message exceeding single-segment limit; far pointers resolve; decode succeeds.
  • Nested structs + lists: depth-N nesting; max pointer depth boundary (pass at N, fail at N+1).
  • Union round-trip: each arm encode → decode → correct discriminant + payload.
  • Schema evolution pair: encode with schemaV1 → decode with schemaV2 (added optional field → reads default).
  • Canonical encoding: two logically-equal messages encoded in different insertion order → byte-identical canonical output.
  • ser-tool inspector: pretty-print known message; wire-diff two messages with one field changed; output is stable.

Differential Testing

  • Cross-impl parity: encode same logical struct → compare against capnproto-rust wire bytes for a shared subset of types; flag divergence.
  • Canonical vs non-canonical: canonical round-trip must equal non-canonical decoded then re-canonicalized.
  • Endian neutrality: encode on LE host → decode on simulated BE offset table (all scalars LE by spec); catch host-endian leakage.
  • Layout stability: run layout computer twice with identical schema; assert byte equality of Layout struct.

Property-Based Testing (proptest / quickcheck)

  • Forall random Schema → layout deterministic (two independent runs produce identical Layout).
  • Forall random valid message → encode → validate → decode → re-encode → byte-equal (round-trip idempotence).
  • Forall random Schema pair (V1 subset of V2) → compat checker reports Compatible.
  • Forall random field sequence → Builder produces message passing validation.
  • Forall SchemaHash → hash(schema_a) == hash(schema_b) iff schema_a structurally equal (collision audit).

Fuzzing (cargo-fuzz / libFuzzer targets)

Target Input Harness goal
fuzz_decode arbitrary bytes as wire message must not panic; only SerError
fuzz_validate arbitrary segments pointer loops, huge counts, misaligned ptrs
fuzz_schema_parse arbitrary IDL text parser never panics; only SchemaInvalid
fuzz_evolve_check two arbitrary schema bytes compat checker terminates, no panic
fuzz_canonical arbitrary valid message canonical re-encode byte-stable
  • Corpus: seed from real messages; regression corpus committed to ser-bench/corpus/.
  • CI: 60-second fuzz runs per target on each PR; nightly 8-hour soak.
  • Sanitizers: AddressSanitizer + UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer on all fuzz targets.
  • unsafe blocks audited: every cast annotated with SAFETY comment; fuzz coverage required for each unsafe site.

Chaos and Fault Injection

  • Truncated buffer (slice off last N bytes) → Truncated or OutOfBounds, never panic.
  • Corrupt pointer offset (flip bits in pointer word) → BadPointer.
  • Max-segment-count exceeded → DepthExceeded or OutOfBounds.
  • Allocator failure injection (custom allocator returning None) → Builder surfaces error, no UB.
  • Schema registry unavailable → decode() without schema falls back to reflective mode or errors cleanly.

Deterministic Replay

  • Capture encode inputs (schema hash + field writes) as an event log; replay produces byte-identical wire output.
  • Used for debugging: repro from telemetry-captured schema_hash + message bytes.
  • ser-tool replay <capture.bin> command.

Soak / Long-Running Tests

  • 24-hour encode/decode loop with jemalloc metrics: assert RSS stays flat (no retained allocations).
  • 1M message round-trips per schema; zero validation failures expected on well-formed input.
  • Thread-safety soak: 32 concurrent encode + decode tasks on shared read-only schema; no data race (miri + TSAN).

Conformance and Compatibility Testing

  • Conformance suite: golden .szs IDL files + expected Layout JSON + expected wire hex; run on every CI build.
  • Backward-compat matrix: test crate versions V_n decoding messages encoded by V_n-1, V_n-2.
  • Forward-compat matrix: V_n-1 reading V_n messages with added optional fields; must return schema default.
  • Schema hash stability: re-parsing same IDL across versions must yield same SchemaHash.

Performance Regression Testing

  • Baseline captured per commit; ser-bench runs in CI on every merge to main.
  • Thresholds: encode throughput ≥ 95% of baseline; decode validation ≥ 95%; zero-copy access latency ≤ 105%.
  • criterion statistical comparison; alert on regression > 2σ outside baseline distribution.
  • Flamegraph diff attached to PR if any benchmark regresses.

Build & Release Engineering

Reproducible Builds

  • Cargo.lock committed; workspace-level [patch] section pinned for all crates.
  • SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH set in CI; binary timestamps clamped to epoch for reproducibility.
  • cargo build --locked enforced in CI; lockfile drift → build failure.
  • cargo vendor snapshot for air-gapped builds; vendor dir committed to release branch.
  • Deterministic codegen: ser-codegen output is function of schema hash only; no timestamp/UUID injection into generated files.

Artifact Signing and Binary Provenance

  • Release binaries (ser-cli, ser-tool) signed with cosign keyless signing (Sigstore); signature stored in OCI registry alongside artifact.
  • SLSA Level 2: GitHub Actions build provenance attestation via slsa-github-generator.
  • Checksum manifest (SHA-256 per artifact) published alongside release.
  • cargo-auditable: embed dependency tree + versions inside binary for post-deployment introspection (rust-audit-info).

SBOM Generation

  • cargo-cyclonedx generates CycloneDX SBOM per crate on every release; attached to GitHub Release as sbom.cdx.json.
  • SBOM includes: all transitive deps, licenses, crate versions, source URLs.
  • SBOM diff between releases surfaced in release notes.

Dependency Auditing and Supply-Chain Verification

  • cargo audit runs on every CI push; blocks merge on RUSTSEC advisories with severity ≥ Medium.
  • cargo deny: license allowlist (MIT/Apache-2.0/ISC only); ban list for known-vulnerable versions; duplicate dep check.
  • Dependabot / RenovateBot: weekly dep update PRs; auto-merge if CI green + no advisory.
  • Sigstore verification for any downloaded toolchain or artifact in CI.

CI/CD Topology

push / PR
  │
  ├─ lint (clippy --deny warnings, rustfmt --check)
  ├─ test-stable  (cargo test --workspace --locked)
  ├─ test-MSRV    (rustc 1.78, cargo test --workspace --locked)
  ├─ miri         (ser-core, ser-decode unsafe blocks)
  ├─ fuzz-short   (60s per target, sanitizers on)
  ├─ bench-compare (criterion vs main baseline, threshold check)
  └─ audit        (cargo audit, cargo deny)

merge to main
  ├─ full fuzz (8h nightly, coverage report)
  ├─ soak test (24h memory + thread-safety)
  ├─ SBOM generation
  └─ docs publish (rustdoc + mdBook)

tag vX.Y.Z
  ├─ reproducible release build (--locked, SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH)
  ├─ SLSA provenance attestation
  ├─ cosign signing
  ├─ artifact upload (GitHub Release + crates.io publish)
  └─ schema registry update (publish canonical golden schemas)

Version Stamping

  • Workspace Cargo.toml version is single source of truth; all crates share major.minor.
  • ser-core::VERSION const set via env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"); embedded in Message frame header (optional field).
  • SchemaHash versioned separately from crate version; schema changes tracked independently.
  • Git tag format: v{MAJOR}.{MINOR}.{PATCH}[-rc.N]; pre-release channel: -rc.N → crates.io --allow-dirty pre-release publish.

Release Channels

Channel Trigger Audience Stability
nightly main merge internal unstable API
rc tag vX.Y.Z-rc.N early adopters API frozen
stable tag vX.Y.Z production semver guaranteed
  • Yanking policy: yank within 24h of RUSTSEC advisory; patch release + advisory published simultaneously.

Release Automation

  • cargo-release orchestrates version bump, changelog generation, tag, publish sequence.
  • Changelog auto-generated from conventional commits (feat:, fix:, perf:, break:).
  • crates.io publish gated on CI green + SBOM attached + provenance verified.
  • Rollback: yanked crates revert to previous stable; schema registry retains all historical schema versions.

Performance Engineering

Benchmarking Strategy

  • Harness: ser-bench crate using criterion (statistical, outlier-resistant); micro + macro benchmarks.
  • Benchmark taxonomy:
Level Scope Metric
Micro single field encode/decode ns/op, MB/s
Struct full struct round-trip ns/op, alloc count
Message multi-segment, nested list MB/s throughput
Comparative vs capnproto-rust, flatbuffers, bincode, prost relative throughput
Regression PR vs main baseline % delta, σ
  • Inputs: synthetic schemas (small/medium/large field counts: 8/64/512); real-world-shaped schemas (network event, analytics row, config blob).
  • Isolation: benchmarks run on dedicated CI runner (pinned CPU, governor=performance, turboboost off, hyperthreading off).
  • Warm vs cold: separate cold-start (first decode of untouched buffer) and warm (L1/L2-resident) benchmarks.

Throughput Targets

M# Path Target
M1 Encode (small struct, 8 fields) ≥ 500 MB/s
M2 Encode (large struct, 512 fields) ≥ 200 MB/s
M3 Validation pass (untrusted buffer) ≥ 1 GB/s
M4 Zero-copy field access (cached buffer) ≤ 5 ns/field
M5 Canonical re-encode (idempotent) ≥ 300 MB/s
M6 Schema parse (IDL → Schema) ≤ 50 ms for 10K-field schema
M7 Arena reuse vs fresh allocation (Phase 10) ≥ 3× speedup
M8 Batch encode of 100× 1 KB messages vs 100 single calls (Phase 10) ≥ 10% faster
M9 Codegen emission for a 50-field struct (Phase 6) < 5 ms
M10 Schema-evolution compat check on a 100-type schema (Phase 7) < 2 ms
M11 ser-tool inspect on a 1 MB message (Phase 12) < 500 ms
M12 ser-tool diff on two 100 KB messages (Phase 12) < 200 ms
  • Regression gate: > 5% decline blocks merge; > 10% requires perf investigation issue. M1–M6 are measured directly by ser-bench; M7–M12 are cross-referenced from their originating phase's N.4 Acceptance Criteria (Phase 10, Phase 6, Phase 7, Phase 12 respectively) so the same number is never asserted twice independently.

Profiling Methodology

  • perf stat + perf record on Linux CI for encode/decode hot paths; annotated disassembly reviewed on regressions.
  • cargo-flamegraph (inferno + perf): generate flamegraph per benchmark; diff flamegraph between commits on regression.
  • valgrind --tool=callgrind for instruction-count-stable profiling (deterministic, reproducible).
  • heaptrack / dhat for allocation profiling; target: zero heap allocs in zero-copy read path post-validation.

Flamegraph Workflow

cargo flamegraph --bench encode_bench -- --bench
  → flamegraph.svg (inferno)
  → committed to artifacts/flamegraphs/{commit_sha}/encode.svg
diff: inferno-diff-folded old.folded new.folded | inferno-flamegraph > diff.svg
  • Flamegraph stored per release tag; diff vs previous tag surfaced in release notes.

Allocator and Lock Analysis

  • Allocator: jemalloc via tikv-jemallocator in bench + production profiles; stats sampled via jemalloc epoch.
  • Target: encode path allocates only into arena (no per-field Vec growth after initial reserve); decode path = zero alloc.
  • Lock contention: ser-runtime arena is per-thread or per-message; no global lock in hot path. Lock analysis via perf lock.
  • Atomic operations: ser-telemetry counters use AtomicU64::fetch_add(Relaxed); no sequentially-consistent fences in encode/decode.

Cache and Syscall Analysis

  • perf stat -e cache-misses,cache-references,LLC-load-misses per benchmark; target LLC miss rate < 2% for warm decode.
  • Layout of Reader<'a, T> sized to fit in two cache lines (pointer + base offset + length).
  • Syscall audit: zero syscalls in encode + zero-copy decode paths (strace -c verified); all I/O via caller-provided &[u8].
  • NUMA: numactl --cpunodebind=0 --membind=0 for bench isolation on multi-socket; NUMA-local allocation documented.

Tail-Latency Analysis

  • p50/p95/p99/p999 reported by criterion via --measurement-time; tail driven by GC-free design.
  • Worst-case validation cost bounded: O(N) in message size, O(D) in pointer depth; both capped → predictable tail.
  • Histogram captured under load via hdrhistogram; published to ser-telemetry metrics endpoint.

Memory Profiling

  • dhat (Valgrind) run on encode + decode of 100K messages; assert peak live bytes ≤ arena size + fixed overhead.
  • RSS monitored in soak test (24h); alert if RSS grows > 10% above steady-state.
  • Stack usage: ensure decode validation stack depth ≤ 32 frames (recursion bounded by DepthExceeded); stacker crate for stack probing if needed.
  • Leaked arena segments: checked via custom allocator wrapper in tests; drop(Builder) must release all segments.

Tracing Methodology

  • tracing crate spans: encode, validate, decode, layout_compute, schema_parse — each a named span.
  • Span fields: schema_hash, message_bytes, segment_count, depth_max.
  • In production: tracing-subscriber with JSON formatter; compatible with OpenTelemetry collector.
  • Benchmark traces: tracing-timing captures per-span histograms for encode/decode; surfaced in ser-telemetry.

Scalability Analysis

Bottleneck Identification

Component Bottleneck Root cause Ceiling
ser-encode Arena segment allocation Vec::extend on segment growth Single-thread ~500 MB/s
ser-decode validation Pointer traversal Random memory access pattern LLC miss rate
ser-schema parse IDL tokenizer Single-threaded parse ~10K fields/ms
ser-layout Offset assignment Sequential field scan Negligible vs parse
ser-evolve compat check Schema graph traversal O(F²) field comparison Amortized by schema hash cache
ser-telemetry Metric sink flush Batched write; bounded queue Drop on overflow (non-blocking)

Horizontal Scaling

  • Stateless encode/decode: no shared mutable state between message processes; horizontally scalable to N workers with linear throughput.
  • Schema is read-only post-parse; share via Arc<Schema> across threads; zero contention on read path.
  • ser-runtime arena is per-message (not global); N concurrent encodes = N independent arenas; no cross-thread locking.
  • Schema registry (external) scales independently; clients cache schemas by SchemaHash; registry reads are read-heavy → replicate freely.

Vertical Scaling

  • Encode throughput scales with CPU frequency + SIMD width (padding-zero operations vectorizable).
  • Decode validation: memory-bandwidth bound on large messages; vertical scaling = larger LLC + higher memory bandwidth.
  • Arena pre-allocation: reserve initial_capacity tuned per workload; reduces realloc calls; single large alloc amortizes segment growth.
  • Parallelism within one message: multi-segment messages can validate segments in parallel (independent bounds checks per segment); rayon parallel iter on segment slice.

Sharding and Partitioning

  • Messages are self-contained; no cross-message dependencies → partition by any key (schema type, source, time).
  • Schema evolution compatibility check is per-schema-pair; shard registry by TypeId namespace.
  • Wire format carries SchemaHash in frame header → routers can partition by schema without deserializing payload.
  • Large list fields: list elements are independently bounded; split list into chunks for parallel processing at application layer (not runtime concern).

Replication Pressure

  • Schema objects are small (~KB for typical schemas); replicate freely to all nodes at startup.
  • Wire messages are opaque byte slices → replicate without decode; consumers decode locally using cached schema.
  • Schema registry replication: eventual consistency acceptable; schema content is immutable once published (append-only evolution).
  • SchemaHash acts as content address; dedup replication by hash.

Coordination Overhead

  • No coordinator required for encode/decode; stateless hot path.
  • Schema negotiation (sender/receiver schema compat check): one-time per connection setup, amortized; ser-evolve check is O(F) where F = field count.
  • Far pointers require in-message cross-segment resolution; all within a single message buffer; no distributed coordination.
  • Schema version advertisement: frame header carries SchemaHash; receiver resolves locally or queries registry once → cache hit path = zero coordination.

Network Fanout

  • Broadcast pattern: encode once → replicate wire bytes N times; no re-encode per recipient.
  • Schema-aware routing: intermediate router inspects frame header (SchemaHash, root Pointer) without full decode; O(1) routing decision.
  • Message framing (segment table in header) enables partial streaming: first segment decodable before all segments received (future capability; mark in blueprint, not Phase-1).
  • Fanout amplification defense: pointer depth + segment count bounds prevent single-message CPU amplification attacks on receivers.

Scheduler Saturation

  • Async-friendly: encode/decode are CPU-bound, non-blocking; safe to run on tokio blocking thread pool or rayon work-stealing pool.
  • Avoid holding arena across .await points; encode to completion, then send wire bytes.
  • Validation pass is bounded O(N) CPU; suitable for tokio blocking task (< 1ms for typical messages < 1MB).
  • Schema parse is one-time startup cost; not on hot path; run on dedicated blocking thread.

Storage Growth

  • Wire messages: size = data_words * 8 + ptr_words * 8 per struct, plus segment framing; no metadata bloat beyond schema-defined fields.
  • Schema registry: schema objects append-only; prune unreferenced schemas after migration window; expect < 1MB per 1000 schema versions.
  • Canonical encoding enables content-addressed dedup storage; identical logical messages → identical bytes → single stored copy.
  • Audit / corpus storage (ser-bench/corpus/): fuzzing corpora grow unboundedly; prune with cargo fuzz cmin periodically.

Metadata Scaling

  • SchemaHash(u64) fits in packet header with negligible overhead; 8 bytes per message.
  • Registry index: HashMap<SchemaHash, Arc<Schema>>; 10K distinct schemas = ~10K entries, O(1) lookup, ~tens of MB.
  • TypeId(u32) namespace: 4B type slots per schema; sufficient for any realistic schema cardinality.
  • FieldId(u16) per type: 65535 fields per struct; typical structs < 256; high-cardinality union types (event taxonomies) approach limit → document as hard ceiling, recommend union-of-union pattern above 1000 arms.
  • Schema evolution history: store only schema diffs (added fields + reserved holes); full history compressible to O(fields * versions) space.

Mental Models & First Principles

MM-1 — Wire format is a typed memory map, not a serialized object. The buffer IS the struct; there is no intermediate representation. Field access is an arithmetic offset read into shared memory. Predicts: any "parse" step is a performance regression; zero-copy decode is the target state. Without this model, engineers add unnecessary deserialization steps. Applied in: Phase 0 (skeleton), Phase 5 (decode), §6.2 Zero-Copy by Default.

MM-2 — Validation is a firewall, not a guard rail. The validation pass separates trusted space (post-validation buffer) from untrusted space (raw wire bytes). After the firewall, no further checking is needed; before it, no cast is safe. Predicts: all security vulnerabilities arise from bypassing or weakening the firewall, not from post-validation accessor bugs. Without this model, engineers add redundant checks in hot-path accessors (slowing them) or skip validation for "performance" (creating vulnerabilities). Applied in: Phase 5, Phase 11, §6.5 Bounded Resource Usage, §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary.

MM-3 — Schema is immutable identity, not mutable configuration. A schema is a content-addressed artifact: its SchemaHash is a commitment to its exact structure. Changing a schema produces a new schema with a new hash. Evolution rules define how reader schemas and writer schemas relate — not how a single schema mutates. Predicts: all compat bugs arise from treating schema change as in-place mutation. Without this model, engineers overwrite schema versions and lose the ability to validate in-flight messages. Applied in: Phase 1, Phase 7, §6.4 Append-Only Schema Evolution.

MM-4 — Layout is a pure function of schema, not of platform. Given the same schema bytes, compute_layout produces byte-identical Layout on every CPU architecture and OS. This means layout is testable, deterministic, and cacheable. Predicts: any layout divergence is a bug in the layout computer, not a platform difference to work around. Without this model, engineers introduce platform-conditioned offsets, breaking cross-platform wire compatibility. Applied in: Phase 2, §7.3 Layout Determinism, I2.

MM-5 — Amplification is a force multiplier for attackers. A 128-byte message that claims a list of 2^32 elements causes O(2^32) work during validation. The severity of amplification scales with the ratio of message bytes to claimed work, not with message size. Predicts: any counter field (count, depth, segment count) that isn't bounds-checked against message size is an amplification vector. Without this model, engineers add count fields without validating that count × element_size ≤ buffer size. Applied in: Phase 5, Phase 11, §6.5, ADR-17, P11 (Cap'n Proto advisory).

MM-6 — Schema evolution is append-only because deletion is physically impossible. Removing a field from a schema does not remove its bytes from existing wire messages. Those bytes exist on disk, in flight, in archives. "Removing" a field must instead declare it reserved — preserving the byte range and offset so surviving fields are not displaced. Predicts: any "remove field" operation without a reserved hole will silently corrupt existing messages read by old code. Without this model, engineers delete field entries and shift offsets. Applied in: Phase 7, Phase 8, §6.4, ADR-07, P10.

MM-7 — Arena allocation is amortization, not optimization. The arena allocator does not make individual allocations faster; it amortizes the overhead of many allocations across a single bump operation. The win is not per-allocation latency but total allocation count. Predicts: arena reuse (reset without free) compounds the win — steady-state operation reaches near-zero allocation cost. Without this model, engineers profile per-alloc speed rather than total allocation count per message. Applied in: Phase 4 (encode), Phase 10 (performance), §8.9 Arena Segment Growth Policy.

MM-8 — Pointer encoding is relative, not absolute. All struct and list pointers encode a word offset relative to the pointer slot's own position, not an absolute address into the segment. This means a serialized message is position-independent: it can be placed at any address in memory, copied to any segment, or memory-mapped from any file offset. Predicts: any absolute-address pointer in the wire format breaks mmap-backed zero-copy. Without this model, engineers use absolute offsets and tie the format to a fixed load address. Applied in: Phase 3 (Wire Format), Phase 4 (encode), §8.2 Wire Format.

Comparative Architecture Table

This design is a struct-oriented, schema-first, zero-copy binary runtime with a single validate-then-view trust boundary and a custom arena allocator. The table below compares it to five real systems across the dimensions that determine behavior in production.

Dimension This Design Cap'n Proto FlatBuffers Protocol Buffers Apache Arrow IPC SBE
Decode model Validate-then-view; zero allocation post-validate Zero-copy view; trust on construction; no mandatory validate Zero-copy view; vtable indirection; optional verifier Full parse+copy; no zero-copy Zero-copy IPC; schema-per-batch; columnar not row Template decode; no pointer indirection; fixed layout
Schema IDL Custom .szs; evolution rules embedded .capnp; unique type IDs per field .fbs; optional fields via vtable; union via enum .proto; field numbers; reserved keyword JSON schema; no IDL file XML template; fixed SBE message template
Pointer type Struct/List/Far (Cap'n Proto-compatible format) Struct/List/Far/Capability; 4 pointer types Table offset + vtable; relative from object start None (parse-only) Buffer offset within record batch None; fixed stride from position
Trust model Explicit validation pass; trust boundary enforced Implicit trust; capability pointers for RPC Optional verifier; not required for decode Parse validates implicitly Schema embedded in IPC stream; no adversarial model Fixed offsets; no dynamic pointers; no validation needed
Evolution model Append-only FieldId; reserved holes; optional defaults Append-only; union arms append; required → optional Append fields with new IDs; deprecated fields kept Field number append; reserved keyword; optional Schema versioned per IPC message; no field-level compat No evolution; template versioning via templateId field
Wire size (small struct) data_words×8 + ptr_words×8; no vtable overhead Same word-aligned layout Vtable overhead per table object (+8–16 bytes) Varint-encoded; compact for small integers Buffer per column; overhead high for small record count Exactly stride × count; most compact for fixed layouts
Language targets Rust + Python (Phase 9); extensible backend C++, Rust, Python, Java, Go, C# C++, Rust, Go, Python, Java, C#, Kotlin, Swift All major languages + grpc ecosystem All major languages; language-agnostic IPC buffer C++, Java, Go, C#; FIX trading community
Amplification defense MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS + MAX_NESTING_DEPTH + MAX_SEGMENTS MAX_NESTING_DEPTH (64); total bytes cap in validator Verifier has max_depth; not on by default Parse bound by message size limit Not an adversarial model No pointers; no amplification possible

Cap'n Proto. Cap'n Proto pioneered the zero-copy struct layout this design adopts verbatim (struct/list/far pointers, 8-byte words, little-endian). The key decision it made differently is the capability pointer (type 3): Cap'n Proto is an RPC protocol at heart, and capabilities are first-class citizens of the wire format. The niche where Cap'n Proto wins is distributed systems with object-capability security and live RPC, where capabilities replace separate authentication layers. This design omits capabilities entirely because the capability model requires a live connection and an object graph — both incompatible with the append-to-disk / mmap-and-read use case.

FlatBuffers. FlatBuffers made the vtable decision: every table object (the FlatBuffers analog of struct) carries a vtable pointer, and the vtable maps field virtual ids to byte offsets. This means optional fields are extremely cheap (absent field = vtable entry 0) and layout does not need to be pre-declared. The niche where FlatBuffers wins is mobile/game clients where schema is tightly controlled, field presence is highly variable, and the vtable's per-object overhead (8–16 bytes) is acceptable relative to message size. This design rejects the vtable because vtable indirection adds a memory access per field read, breaking sequential-scan performance on dense structs.

Protocol Buffers. Protocol Buffers chose varint encoding and a full parse step. Every decode allocates memory, parses tag-type-value triples, and builds an in-memory object graph. The niche where Protocol Buffers wins is the general-purpose, cross-organization API contract scenario: the ecosystem depth (grpc, protoc plugins, reflection, JSON interop, Any type) is unmatched. When wire efficiency matters less than developer ergonomics and ecosystem breadth, Proto wins. This design explicitly rejects Proto's parse model because the zero-copy property requires fixed field offsets at known positions — impossible with varints and tag-value framing.

Apache Arrow IPC. Arrow is columnar: a record batch stores all values of one field contiguously across rows, enabling SIMD-accelerated analytics over columns. Arrow's IPC format achieves zero-copy by memory-mapping contiguous buffer regions per column. The niche where Arrow wins is analytical queries (GROUP BY, aggregation, vectorized filter) over millions of rows, where columnar access dominates. This design is row-oriented (one struct = one record = all fields together), which serves random-access read of individual records with minimal data touched. The two models are complementary, not competing: Arrow for batch analytics, this runtime for individual message serialization.

SBE (Simple Binary Encoding). SBE eliminates all pointer indirection: fields are at fixed strides from the message start, determined by the template. There is no validation pass because there are no pointers to follow. The niche where SBE wins is ultra-low-latency fixed-schema financial messaging (FIX protocol), where every nanosecond of decode latency matters and the schema is contractually frozen between counterparties. SBE's inability to evolve schema without a breaking change is a feature in that context: stable contracts are enforced structurally. This design rejects SBE's model because the target use case requires schema evolution across independently deployed services.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All derivations start from the System Constraints table (§4.1). Notation: 1 GiB = 2^30 bytes; 1 MiB = 2^20 bytes; 1 word = 8 bytes.

1. Encode throughput ceiling (single-thread arena path)

System constraint: target encode throughput = 1 GiB/s.

  • Arena bump allocator: 1 pointer increment + 1 zeroing pass per word written.
  • Memory bandwidth requirement: 1 GiB/s output means 1 GiB/s of memset-equivalent work on the arena slab.
  • Modern cores: ~40–80 GiB/s L1/L2 bandwidth, ~25 GiB/s L3 bandwidth.
  • At 1 GiB/s, encode is comfortably within L3 bandwidth for small messages (< 256 KB fit in LLC).
  • Multi-segment overhead: each segment boundary triggers 1 far-pointer write (8 bytes) + segment header update; at typical segment size 4 KB = 512 words, overhead = 1/512 = 0.2% — negligible.
  • Result: 1 GiB/s encode is achievable on a single core against L3 cache. Sustained throughput across many cores: limited by DRAM bandwidth (~50 GiB/s dual-channel) → up to ~50 concurrent encoding cores before memory is the bottleneck.

2. Decode (validate-then-view) throughput ceiling

Validation pass: visits every word in the message once (O(N) pointer traversal).

  • 1 GiB/s decode target.
  • Traversal cost = 1 cache-line load per 8-word block = 1 load per 64 bytes.
  • At 1 GiB/s, decode reads ~15.6 million 64-byte cache lines per second.
  • L3 hit rate for messages < 8 MB: high (most messages fit in LLC of 8–32 MB).
  • L3 latency: ~10 ns; 15.6 M × 10 ns = 156 ms/s of pure latency → throughput ceiling ~6.4 GiB/s at L3 latency (sequentially reads are pipelined by hardware prefetch).
  • Result: validation at 1 GiB/s is memory-bandwidth limited, not latency limited. Hardware prefetch makes sequential scan near L3-bandwidth optimal (~30–60 GiB/s theoretical; practical 6–10 GiB/s after pointer chasing).

3. Amplification attack budget

Constraint: MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES = 512 MiB (I10).

  • Worst-case amplification: list pointer claiming 2^29 elements of 1 word each = 2^29 × 8 = 4 GiB → rejected immediately (> MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES).
  • With MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES check: any list count × element_size > 512 MiB → rejected in O(1) before traversal.
  • Remaining amplification: nested struct traversal; MAX_NESTING_DEPTH = 64 (I6); MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS bounds total words visited.
  • MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS = MAX_MESSAGE_BYTES / 8 = 64 Mi words = 67,108,864 words.
  • Traversal budget per validation: 64 Mi word visits × 1 ns per visit = 67 ms. This is the worst-case validation latency for a 512 MiB message.
  • Result: amplification is bounded. A 128-byte message cannot cause more than 128/8 = 16 word visits (bounds check rejects count > buffer size). The ratio of work to message bytes is O(1).

4. Schema parse cost per service startup

Typical schema: 100 types × 20 fields average = 2000 fields.

  • IDL token rate: ~10 MB/s parse (conservative; serial PEG parser).
  • Typical .szs file: 2000 fields × ~50 bytes per field declaration = ~100 KB.
  • Parse time: 100 KB / 10 MB/s = 10 ms.
  • Hash computation: SipHash-1-3 over ~10 KB canonical bytes: < 0.1 ms.
  • Result: schema parse is a one-time 10–15 ms startup cost. Not on hot path. Safe to run synchronously during service initialization.

5. Schema registry memory footprint

10,000 distinct schema versions deployed across an organization (large org estimate).

  • Per schema: ~2000 fields × 64 bytes per Field struct = ~128 KB per schema (in-memory).
  • 10K schemas: 10K × 128 KB = 1.28 GB. This is too large for a per-service in-memory cache.
  • Practical model: cache most-recently-used 100 schemas; 100 × 128 KB = 12.8 MB → well within per-service budget.
  • SchemaHash→Schema map overhead: 10K entries × 32 bytes (hash + pointer) = 320 KB for the index.
  • Result: per-service cache of 100 schemas costs ~13 MB. Full in-process registry for large orgs requires demand-loading from persistent store.

6. Fuzzing corpus size vs bug discovery rate

Empirical (libFuzzer behavior): coverage-guided fuzzing plateau for a 2 KLOC validation function is typically reached at ~100K unique corpus entries.

  • Corpus entry: average 256 bytes (message-like structure with valid/invalid pointer patterns).
  • Total corpus size: 100K × 256 bytes = ~25 MB.
  • Daily CI fuzz run: 30 minutes × 10^6 executions/min (modern libFuzzer rate) = 1.8 × 10^9 executions/day.
  • At 30-minute CI runs, the fuzz target explores ~54 million paths/day.
  • Result: 30-minute daily fuzz runs are meaningful for input coverage. 25 MB corpus is acceptable for commit to repository. Continuous fuzzing (separate from CI) recommended for security-critical targets.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Q1. If a caller caches a Reader<'a, T> across the buffer's lifetime, Rust's borrow checker prevents the bug. But what happens with raw FFI callers that bypass lifetime tracking — which invariants in §7 prevent use-after-free? Answer synthesizes: §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary (the validated buffer must outlive all readers — contract enforced by caller, not runtime) + §6.8 Safe Abstractions Over Unsafe Internals (I5: no Reader is created without a valid validated message reference; FFI callers must honor the equivalent of 'a lifetime explicitly). The buffer-outlives-reader contract is an FFI caller responsibility, not a runtime enforcement. The spec must document this explicitly.

Q2. The canonical encoding mode (P5, §8.15 Canonical Encoding) guarantees byte-identical output for logically-equal messages. But what does "logically equal" mean for a union type where the active arm has different padding in different schema versions? Answer synthesizes: §6.4 Append-Only Schema Evolution (reserved holes preserve layout) + §8.11 Schema Evolution Worked Example (canonical form is defined per writer schema — two messages encoded under different schema versions may be logically equal under the application model but not byte-identical under canonical encoding). The canonical guarantee is per-schema-version, not cross-version.

Q3. The amplification defense (§6.5) caps traversal at MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS. But far pointers (§8.2) can form a chain — can a chain of N far pointers all within the word budget still cause O(N) heap allocations? Answer synthesizes: §8.2 Wire Format far-pointer spec (far pointer resolution follows one indirection — double-far pointer follows exactly two hops, not a chain) + §6.5 Bounded Resource Usage (each hop visits exactly 2 words — the landing pad; total work = 2 × number_of_far_ptrs ≤ MAX_TRAVERSAL_WORDS). Far pointers cannot chain beyond double-far; there is no "chain of N far pointers." The spec must document this explicitly to prevent implementation confusion.

Q4. The schema hash (ADR-08) uses xxHash3, which is non-cryptographic. A malicious schema server could send a crafted schema with the same hash as a legitimate one. What defense exists, and when is SHA-256 required? Answer synthesizes: ADR-08 (xxHash3 for non-crypto use; SHA-256 for security boundaries) + §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary (schema hash is a compatibility identifier, not a capability token; the trust model assumes the schema registry is trusted infrastructure). If the registry is untrusted (public internet, adversarial tenants), the schema hash becomes a security boundary and SHA-256 is required. ADR-08 explicitly calls this out as the "When the Rejected Choice Wins" case.

Q5. ListReader::get(idx) is bounds-checked against count (Phase 5). But the validation pass already confirmed list bounds. Is this a double-check, and does it violate the zero-copy / no-redundant-work design principle? Answer synthesizes: §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary (validation establishes that all element offsets are in-bounds; post-validation accessor reads are total) + §6.2 Zero-Copy by Default (accessor bounds check against count is an index-out-of-range guard, not a buffer-bounds guard). The validation pass proves buffer bounds; the accessor's count check proves index validity — two different invariants. The count check is O(1) and not redundant; it is the right layer for index-out-of-range errors.

Q6. The layout computer (Phase 2) packs fields by alignment descending to minimize padding. But the canonical encoding pass (Phase 9) writes fields in FieldId ascending order. Can these two orders conflict in a way that produces non-deterministic padding? Answer synthesizes: Phase 2 (layout assigns byte offsets independently of write order) + Phase 9 (canonical encoding writes data to pre-determined offsets, not recomputing them). Layout is a pure function of schema; offsets are fixed before encoding begins. Write order does not affect padding because padding bytes are zeroed regardless of when they are written. The two orders operate on different abstractions: layout on offsets, canonical encoding on write sequence.

Q7. The ser-evolve compat checker (Phase 7) verifies backward/forward compat between two schema versions. What is the O() complexity of the check and what is the failure mode if a schema has 10,000 fields? Answer synthesizes: Phase 7 (compat check is O(F) for field count per type, O(T×F) total for T types) + §4.1 System Constraints (field count per schema bounded by FieldId u16 range = 65,535 max; practical limit lower). For a schema with 10,000 fields, compat check is ~10,000 comparisons — sub-millisecond. Cache schema pairs by (hash_a, hash_b) to avoid recomputing on repeated calls.

Q8. Far pointers enable messages to span multiple segments (§8.2). In a multi-segment message, can the validation pass visit the same segment more than once? What prevents a cycle? Answer synthesizes: Phase 5 (validation tracks visited words via BitSet; any word re-visited → BadPointer) + §6.5 (MAX_SEGMENTS = 512 caps segment count). A segment can legitimately be referenced by multiple pointers, but each word within it can only be the target of one pointer (enforced by the visited-words BitSet). Cycles are detected when a far-pointer landing pad points back to an already-visited word.

Q9. The Builder pool (Phase 10) caps at MAX_POOL_SIZE (default 64) builders. What happens under burst traffic where 200 concurrent goroutines/tasks request builders simultaneously? Answer synthesizes: Phase 10 (pool miss path: acquire() creates a new Builder when pool is empty — not a blocking call) + §4.1 (encode latency target 1 µs/KB). A pool miss allocates a new Builder on the fly. The pool is an optimization (reduces allocation per message in steady state), not a semaphore. Under burst load, extra builders are allocated and discarded after use (not returned to full pool). Latency impact: single allocation of arena slab — sub-microsecond.

Q10. The codegen path (Phase 6) emits Rust source code. If the generated code contains a bug in an offset constant, that bug is invisible at compile time. What is the verification strategy? Answer synthesizes: Phase 6 (generated accessors are derived from Layout constants, which are tested in layout_golden.rs) + Phase 13 (differential harness verifies generated Reader against reflective Reader for the same schema). The golden test catches layout divergence at codegen time; the differential benchmark catches runtime divergence. No offset constant in generated code is hand-written — all derived from Layout via emit_accessor(field.offset, field.ty).

Q11. Text fields (WireType::Text, Phase 8) are validated as UTF-8 during the validation pass. The accessor then uses from_utf8_unchecked. What happens if a future version of the validation pass skips the UTF-8 check as an "optimization"? Answer synthesizes: §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary (I5: from_utf8_unchecked is only safe under the post-validation guarantee that UTF-8 check was performed) + ADR-17 (unsafe policy: every unsafe block must carry a SAFETY comment naming the invariant it relies on). The SAFETY: comment on from_utf8_unchecked must cite I8 (UTF-8 validated in validation pass). Any future optimization removing the UTF-8 check must break the SAFETY: contract visibly, causing a mandatory audit.

Q12. The schema IDL supports union types. A union's active arm is identified by a u16 discriminant in the data section (Phase 8). What prevents a writer from encoding discriminant 5 but writing the data of arm 3? Answer synthesizes: Phase 6 (codegen emits typed set_arm_X() methods on UnionBuilder that both set the discriminant and write the arm's data in one atomic operation) + Phase 7 (evolution: new arms appended; old discriminants never reused). The Builder API makes mismatched discriminant/data physically impossible through the typed API. The validation pass does not separately validate discriminant-data consistency (it is a structural, not semantic, check) — the correctness guarantee is in the codegen layer.

Q13. ser-telemetry uses tracing spans for distributed pipeline correlation (Phase 12). What happens to span context when a message crosses a process boundary (encode in process A, decode in process B)? Answer synthesizes: Phase 12 (span context is injected via TelemetryContext; not embedded in the wire format) + §6.15 Explicit Message Framing, No Magic Bytes (wire format carries only schema hash and data — no tracing metadata). Span context propagation across processes is out of scope for this runtime; it is an application-layer concern (e.g., inject trace ID into a dedicated metadata field in the application schema). The runtime does not inject or extract distributed tracing context from wire messages.

Q14. The differential benchmark (Phase 13) compares this runtime against bincode on round-trip correctness. If bincode round-trips correctly but this runtime returns a different value for a float64 field containing NaN, which is "correct"? Answer synthesizes: Phase 13 (differential asserts field equality via PartialEq) + §4.1 System Constraints + ADR-04 (little-endian fixed). IEEE 754 NaN is not equal to itself under PartialEq. The differential harness must use bits() == bits() for float comparisons, not ==. This is a correctness trap in the differential test design, not a bug in the serialization format. The format passes through float64 bits verbatim (fixed-width LE).

Q15. The Deferred Features Register includes mmap-backed zero-copy read (DF7). What invariants from the current design must remain stable to un-defer this feature without a breaking change? Answer synthesizes: §7.2 Validation Trust Boundary (I5: post-validation safety) + Phase 5 (validation pass must accept a &[u8] slice view, regardless of backing store — mmap or heap) + §8.2 Wire Format (relative pointers → position-independent format). The format is already mmap-compatible: relative pointers, fixed layout, no absolute addresses. Un-deferring requires only providing an mmap-backed &[u8] to validate() — no format change needed. The invariant that must remain stable: validation input is a contiguous &[u8] slice (I5 pre-condition).

Q16. If two engineers independently write the same struct in two different .szs files with the same TypeId, what happens at schema registry time and at decode time? Answer synthesizes: Phase 1 (validate.rs rejects duplicate TypeId within one schema) + Phase 12 (schema registry maps SchemaHash → Schema; two different schema files with the same TypeId but different structures have different SchemaHash values). There is no collision at registry time — they are distinct schemas. At decode time, if a message encoded under schema A is decoded with schema B (same TypeId but different layout), ser-evolve compat check returns IncompatibleSchema. The defense is: SchemaHash is a commit to the full schema structure, not just the TypeId.

Q17. The packed scalar list (ADR-30, Phase 8) requires a decode pass into a temporary allocation. This breaks the zero-allocation guarantee on the accessor path. Is this a design inconsistency? Answer synthesizes: ADR-30 (packed mode is opt-in; explicitly noted as incompatible with zero-copy) + §6.2 Zero-Copy by Default (zero-copy is the default; packed lists are a documented exception accessible only through an explicitly different API surface). The design is consistent: the default path is zero-copy and zero-allocation; the packed API is a separate opt-in for bandwidth-constrained scenarios with explicit allocation semantics. The API surface enforces the distinction — there is no ambiguity at call sites.

Q18. What is the minimum-correct implementation of this runtime — the smallest subset of crates that can encode and decode a single fixed-schema struct without evolution, codegen, or telemetry? Answer synthesizes: Phase 0 (ser-core skeleton), Phase 3 (ser-layout), Phase 4 (ser-encode), Phase 5 (ser-decode). The minimum-correct implementation is: ser-core (types + wire format) + ser-schema (parse) + ser-layout (offset assignment) + ser-encode (Builder + Arena) + ser-decode (validate + Reader). Six crates. No ser-codegen (use reflective Reader), no ser-evolve (single schema version), no ser-telemetry (no metrics), no ser-tool, no ser-bench, no ser-cli. This is the "phase 0–5 bootstrap" and is the target deliverable for the first milestone gate.

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Alt-1 — Interpreted/Reflective-Only (No Arena, No Codegen)

+--------+     parse IDL      +--------+     interpret      +--------+
| .szs   | -----------------> | Schema | ----------------> | Reader |
| source |                    | (heap) |     at runtime     | (dyn)  |
+--------+                    +--------+                    +--------+
         all field accesses: schema lookup + offset compute per call

Architecture: keep schema in memory as a runtime graph; each field access looks up the schema graph to find the offset, then performs a bounds check and cast. No code generation. No arena allocator — use system heap per-struct.

Regime where it wins: dynamic scripting environments (Python plugins, REPL tools, schema-less consumers) where the schema is not known at compile time and type-safety is irrelevant. Also correct for prototyping where schema churn is high.

Real system that embodies it: ser-tool's reflective reader (the reflective fallback path already in this design); also rkyv's Archived without codegen; also Protocol Buffers' DynamicMessage API.

Why rejected as primary design: field access cost is O(schema_lookup) + O(offset_compute) per access rather than O(1) compiled constant. For a 10-field struct accessed 10 million times per second, schema lookup dominates. The codegen path reduces this to a single integer addition.

Alt-2 — Vtable-Per-Object (FlatBuffers-style)

+--------+   allocate vtable  +-------+  vtable pointer   +--------+
| Builder| -----------------> |Vtable | <---------------- | Object |
|        |   (per-object,     | [off] |                   | header |
|        |    heap or arena)  +-------+                   +--------+
         each field access: deref vtable[virtual_id] -> offset -> data

Architecture: every encoded object carries a vtable pointer. The vtable maps virtual field IDs to byte offsets. Fields absent in a given message have vtable entry 0. Layout is computed at encode time, not schema time.

Regime where it wins: highly optional field presence (sparse messages where most fields are absent most of the time); backward compat without any layout pre-declaration; mobile/game clients where schema version churn is high and vtable overhead (8–16 bytes per object) is acceptable.

Real system that embodies it: FlatBuffers (table objects carry vtable offset in the first word; vtable is builder-allocated per distinct field set).

Why rejected: vtable adds one memory indirection per field read. For a cache-line-resident struct of 8 fields, the vtable dereference is a separate cache-line load, doubling memory traffic. The schema-first layout model of this design makes vtables unnecessary: all offsets are known at compile/schema-parse time.

Alt-3 — Copy-on-Decode (Protobuf/Serde-style)

+--------+   full parse pass  +--------+   field access    +--------+
| wire   | -----------------> |  Heap  | ----------------> | owned  |
| bytes  |   (alloc per       | object |   (direct field   | struct |
|        |    field/string)   |        |    in heap struct) |        |
+--------+                    +--------+                    +--------+
         decode = parse + alloc + copy; no validation separate from parse

Architecture: decode is a single-pass operation that simultaneously parses the wire format and builds an owned in-memory struct. Each field is individually allocated (strings as String, bytes as Vec<u8>, nested structs as Box<Nested>). Field access after decode is O(1) struct field access — no wire-format involvement.

Regime where it wins: when messages are frequently processed (many fields accessed repeatedly after decode), when fields are mutated after decode, or when the application model requires owned data with arbitrary lifetimes (across .await points, across thread boundaries, stored in data structures). Protocol Buffers, serde + bincode, Avro with code-generated structs.

Why rejected: zero-copy is the stated design goal. Copy-on-decode is structurally incompatible: no amount of optimization reduces copy-on-decode to zero allocation. The target use case (decode and inspect a message once, then route or discard the buffer) is served by zero-copy with zero allocation; copy-on-decode adds O(message_size) allocation and copy overhead.

Alt-4 — Fixed-Stride, No-Pointer (SBE-style)

+--------+   template lookup  +-------+  stride offset    +--------+
| header | -----------------> | SBE   | <---------------- | field  |
| tempId |                    |Template|  (no pointer,    | access |
|        |                    | (XML) |   no validation)  |        |
+--------+                    +-------+                   +--------+
         decode = template[templateId] -> stride arithmetic; no validation needed

Architecture: the wire format carries a templateId that identifies a fixed message template. All fields are at compile-time-fixed byte offsets from the message start. No pointer types, no variable-length fields in the base template (variable-length fields appended as a "group" with a block length header). No validation pass needed because there are no pointers to validate.

Regime where it wins: ultra-low-latency fixed-schema financial messaging (FIX/FASTs); decode latency in nanoseconds; schema contractually frozen between counterparties; no schema evolution required between releases. SBE is deployed on trading floors where single-digit-nanosecond decode paths are critical.

Real system that embodies it: SBE (Simple Binary Encoding), used in the FIX trading community and the London Metal Exchange.

Why rejected: no pointer types means no variable-length strings, no nested structs, no recursive types. The target use case requires rich type support (Text, Bytes, nested structs, List<T>) and schema evolution. SBE's fixed-stride model is correct only when the message structure is known to both parties at link time and never changes.

Deferred Features Register

Feature Anchor Building Phase Un-defer Trigger
Additional codegen targets (C, Python, Go bindings beyond Phase 9 Python sketch) PhaseDeferred("DF1") Phase 6 (codegen) complete Confirmed demand from ≥2 downstream language teams; CodegenBackend trait stabilized
Remote/cloud schema registry adapter trait PhaseDeferred("DF2") Phase 12 (observability) complete Production deployment requiring cross-service schema resolution; local file-based registry proves insufficient at scale
Packed-scalar-list varint packing (opt-in pack() API) PhaseDeferred("DF3") Phase 8 (complex types) complete Mobile/IoT workload profiling shows wire size dominates over decode latency; bandwidth cost measured > compute cost
Streaming/incremental encode for large messages (> 512 MiB) PhaseDeferred("DF4") Phase 10 (performance) complete Confirmed large-message workload (media, ML model weights) requiring streaming without holding full message in memory
WASM target (wasm32-unknown-unknown + wasm32-wasi) PhaseDeferred("DF5") Phase 9 (cross-platform) complete Browser or edge-compute deployment scenario confirmed; WASM toolchain maturity validated
Wire-level encryption/signing envelope (AEAD wrapper around message bytes) PhaseDeferred("DF6") Phase 11 (safety hardening) complete Regulatory or security requirement for message authenticity at the transport layer; application-layer signing proven insufficient
mmap-backed zero-copy read (MmapReader over file-backed buffers) PhaseDeferred("DF7") Phase 5 (decode) complete Storage-layer use case confirmed (log files, append-only archives > 1 GB) where heap-copy of file contents is too costly

Glossary

Term Definition
Zero-copy read Decode = validate-then-view; field access performs NO heap allocation and NO byte copy. Explicit deep-copy/canonicalize is opt-in only.
Validation trust boundary The single validation pass where ALL bounds, alignment, pointer-target, count, and recursion-depth checks occur. Post-validation accessor reads are total — cannot OOB or misalign. Unvalidated buffers are never accessed.
Reader<'a, T> Zero-copy borrowed view over &'a [u8]; holds buf + base: Offset; accessors cast into the buffer using 'a lifetime; no allocation, no parse.
Builder<'a, T> Arena-backed write handle for type T; allocates into an owned Arena; emits a Message on finish.
Schema { types: Vec<TypeDef>, root: TypeId, schema_hash: SchemaHash } — complete type graph for one logical namespace.
SchemaHash u64 fingerprint computed deterministically from type ids, field ids, field types, and layout; used for compat checking and wire tagging.
TypeDef One type in the schema: `{ id, name, kind: Struct
Field { id: FieldId, name, ty: WireType, offset: Offset, default: DefaultVal, since_version: u32 } — immutable once published.
TypeId u32 globally unique identifier for a type within a schema; stable across schema versions.
FieldId u16 stable identifier for a field within its type; used for evolution/compat matching; never reused.
Offset u32 byte-offset within a segment's data array; always a multiple of the field's alignment requirement.
SegmentId u32 index into Message::segments; segment 0 is the root segment.
WireType Enum of representable wire kinds: Void, Bool, I8–I64, U8–U64, F32, F64, Enum, Struct, List, Union, Text, Bytes, Pointer.
Word 8-byte unit; all offsets and size fields in the wire format are expressed in words.
Layout { data_words: u16, ptr_words: u16, align: u8 } — struct footprint in the data section and pointer section. Layout is a pure function of schema.
Data section First data_words * 8 bytes of a struct's allocation; holds scalar fields packed by offset.
Pointer section Immediately follows the data section; ptr_words * 8 bytes holding Pointer values pointing to variable-length or nested objects.
Struct pointer Pointer::Struct { offset, data_words, ptr_words } — relative word offset from pointer slot to target struct; carries inline layout for validation.
List pointer Pointer::List { offset, elem, count } — points to a sequence of count elements of wire type elem.
Far pointer Pointer::Far { seg, offset } — crosses segment boundaries; resolver must dereference into segment seg at word offset. Amplification-safe: max-segments limit applies.
Segment { id: SegmentId, bytes: Vec<u8> } — contiguous allocation arena; a Message has 1..N segments.
Arena Bump allocator backing a Builder session; grows by adding new segments; never frees mid-build.
Message framing 4-byte segment count + per-segment 4-byte size prefix, little-endian; enables prefixing over any byte stream.
Packed encoding Optional compression of wire bytes: runs of zero bytes replaced with tag byte + nonzero bytes; packing is a post-encode filter, not a format change.
Little-endian All scalar wire values stored in little-endian byte order; fixed for all platforms; accessors on big-endian hosts perform byte-swap on read.
IDL / .szs Interface Description Language source file (extension .szs); parsed by ser-schema; defines structs, enums, unions, lists, and field defaults.
Layout determinism Identical schema → byte-identical layout on every platform; layout is a pure function of TypeDef; no runtime variation.
Canonical encoding Encoding mode yielding byte-identical output for logically-equal messages: fixed field order, zeroed padding, no dead space; enables hashing and deduplication.
Validation pass Single sequential scan of all message bytes performed by ser-decode before any field access; checks bounds, alignment, pointer targets, depth, and segment count.
Amplification attack Crafted message exploiting far-pointer chains or list counts to cause superlinear work; mitigated by total-bytes cap, max-segment count, and recursion-depth limit.
Recursion depth limit Hard cap (default 64) on pointer-following depth during validation; prevents stack overflow on adversarial pointer cycles.
Schema evolution Rules governing backward/forward compat across schema versions: append-only field ids, optional fields default to zero/null, union arms append-only, removed fields become reserved holes.
Reserved hole Byte range left in a struct's data section after a field is logically removed; ensures offsets of surviving fields are not disturbed.
Forward compatibility Old reader can consume message written by new writer; unrecognized fields are silently ignored; unknown union arms present as Unknown variant.
Backward compatibility New reader can consume message written by old writer; absent fields read their schema-declared defaults.
since_version u32 on each Field; the schema version at which the field was added; readers with older schema treat the field as absent.
DefaultVal Per-field encoded default used when an absent or zeroed field is accessed; stored in schema, not in the wire message.
Accessor Generated or reflective method on Reader<'a, T> that returns a field value or a nested Reader; always borrows from the message buffer.
Offset table / vtable Optional indirection table (FlatBuffers-style) mapping field ids to offsets; used in union/evolving structs where layout cannot be fully static.
Union tag / discriminant A u16 stored in the data section identifying the active arm of a Union type.
Text Wire type for UTF-8 strings: pointer-section entry pointing to a byte list prefixed with length; NUL-terminated for C interop.
Bytes Wire type for opaque byte blobs; like Text but no NUL termination, no UTF-8 requirement.
Packed scalar list List whose elements are primitive scalars stored contiguously without per-element pointers; stride = element size.
Varint Variable-length integer encoding (used in protobuf contrast); NOT used in this format — all scalars are fixed-width for zero-copy alignment.
Endianness Byte order of multi-byte scalars on the wire; this runtime fixes little-endian; accessors on BE hosts emit to_le/from_le intrinsics.
Reflective fallback Dynamic field access via Schema + TypeDef at runtime without generated code; used by ser-tool and for schema-less consumers.
ser-telemetry tap Hook points in encode/validate/decode paths that emit tracing spans and metrics counters without altering correctness.
Differential benchmark Benchmark in ser-bench that compares this runtime against capnproto-rust, flatbuffers, and rkyv on identical payloads; regression gate.
Schema registry Service/store in ser-tool mapping SchemaHash → Schema; enables wire-diff and reflective decode of archived messages.
SerError enum { SchemaInvalid, OutOfBounds, Misaligned, BadPointer, DepthExceeded, Truncated, IncompatibleSchema } — exhaustive error taxonomy; all public APIs return Result<_, SerError>.
MSRV Minimum Supported Rust Version = 1.78; governs which stable features are permitted; enforced by rust-version field in workspace Cargo.toml.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 — Skeleton 12-crate workspace compiles clean; hand-coded fixed struct (FooFixed) built into a byte buffer and read back via Reader cast (no schema, no pointers, single segment) 1 week Crate dependency-graph cycles; stub-crate compile errors cargo build --workspace + cargo clippy -D warnings clean; phase0_skeleton integration test passes
1 — Schema/IDL front-end ser-schema: lexer.rs/parser.rs/resolver.rs/validator.rs/hasher.rs; parse_and_validate(idl) -> Result<Schema, SchemaError> public API 2 weeks Recursive-descent grammar ambiguity; SchemaHash non-determinism from HashMap iteration order All 7 fixture test files pass (struct/enum/union/list parse, forward-ref, cycle detection, hash determinism)
2 — Layout computation ser-layout: compute_layout(&Schema) -> Layout; offset assignment, data/ptr-section split, union/list layout 1 week Non-determinism across field sort orders; cross-arch alignment edge cases Golden-file layout tests pass on x86-64 + aarch64; determinism test bit-identical across 2 runs
3 — Wire format + pointers ser-core wire primitives (LE read/write, varint) + ser-runtime segment/arena/framing; struct/list/far Pointer encoding frozen 1 week Offset sign-extension bugs (i30); far-pointer double-indirection confusion; segment-table padding miscount ser-tool wire-dump round-trips all pointer kinds + framing fixtures byte-for-byte
4 — Builder/encode path ser-encode: Arena, Builder<T>, struct/list/text writers, canonical (deterministic) encoding mode 2 weeks Arena growth invalidating in-flight offsets; canonical-mode idempotency regressions Encode → decode round-trips for all WireType variants; canonical output bitwise identical across 100K proptest iters
5 — Zero-copy decode + validation ser-decode: validation pass (bounds/alignment/pointer-target/depth) as sole trust boundary; Reader<'a,T> zero-copy accessors 2 weeks Validation gap allowing post-trust OOB/UB; false-positive rejection of valid messages All malformed-fixture corpus returns correct SerError; zero-heap-allocation assertion passes; 1M-iter fuzz clean
6 — Code generation ser-codegen: per-type Reader/Builder accessor emitter (Rust), reflective fallback path, trybuild compile test 2 weeks Generated code fails to compile; reflective-vs-generated accessor value divergence Generated Rust compiles with 0 clippy warnings; reflective accessor values match generated accessor values on all fixtures
7 — Schema evolution + compatibility ser-evolve: append-only compat checker, CompatReport, version graph, migration defaults 1 week Incomplete evolution-rule coverage; false-compatible verdicts on breaking changes Compat matrix (all valid/invalid evolution ops) passes; ser-cli evolve check exits correct code
8 — Variable-width + advanced types Strings/bytes/nested lists/list-of-struct/maps/packed scalar lists/optional-nullable/default-value elision, zero-copy read_text/read_bytes 2 weeks Nested-list depth mishandling past MAX_NESTING_DEPTH; map key-collision on binary search validate_nested_list rejects depth overflow with DepthExceeded; map round-trip (1000 entries) all values correct
9 — Cross-language + endianness + conformance Canonical-form interop fixture suite (≥20 schemas); Python codegen backend reads Rust-encoded bytes 1 week Cross-platform SchemaHash/layout mismatch; endianness bugs surfacing only on BE targets CI matrix (x86-64/aarch64/wasm32) produces identical fixture SHA-256; Python reader round-trip test passes
10 — Performance Arena reuse/pooling, batching, SIMD-friendly alignment option; full ser-bench criterion suite with CI regression gate 2 weeks Benchmark measurement noise/methodology bias; pooled-arena reuse correctness under reset-not-free arena_reuse bench ≥3× fresh Arena; CI fails on >10% throughput regression from stored baseline
11 — Safety hardening Untrusted-buffer validation depth/segment/byte-count limits; safe_cast<T> as sole unsafe site; fuzz-driven bounds hardening 2 weeks Fuzzer discovers a validation gap; MIRI-detected UB in the unsafe cast path cargo miri test -p ser-decode 0 UB findings; 1-hour fuzz run 0 crashes; all other crates #[forbid(unsafe_code)]
12 — Observability + tooling ser-telemetry (metrics/tracing/Prometheus export) + ser-tool (inspector, wire-diff, schema registry) 2 weeks Telemetry overhead measurable in the hot path; exporter build-dependency bloat Telemetry-off overhead = 0 ns (verified by asm inspection); ser-tool inspect/diff produce correct golden output
13 — Conformance, differential, fuzzing, benchmarks Differential harness vs Serde/bincode + Cap'n Proto/FlatBuffers semantics; validator fuzzing; release-readiness gate 1 week Differential-harness false negatives; benchmark methodology bias masking a regression 10,000-case differential proptest 0 mismatches; cargo publish --dry-run succeeds; CI benchmark gate green
